#### c) The hydrogeology of the receiving environment (joint study Chair of Urban Hydrogeology of UQAM – COBAMIL)
- Permeability: “Very high permeability of sediments in the recharge zone”.
- Hydrogeological connectivity: “Very good water table-lake response” concerning Lake Fauvel.
- Terminal vulnerability: Point Zéro Lake constitutes a “terminal system without outlet”, classified
-   “particularly vulnerable” to contaminant inputs.
- Toxicity modeling: The modeling submitted in the file indicates that a discharge of 2.0 mg/L of
-   cadmium would produce a concentration 180 times higher than the chronic aquatic toxicity criterion
-   in the stream.

#### d) The discharge pipe
A physical pipe located behind the industrial facility would discharge unmonitored effluent into the
road ditch connected to Lockheed Creek and then to Lake Fauvel. The complaint contends that it would
not be incorporated into the department's official sampling protocol.

#### e) Self-monitoring — the alleged conflict of interest
The complaint develops a grievance of self-surveillance: the private Stablex laboratory is
accredited by the Quebec ministry; sampling at observation wells is carried out jointly due to lack
of independent ministerial equipment; and an alleged practice of prior pumping of wells would dilute
the samples, leading to divergent Ministry/Stablex results for the same well. The author refers to
section 272 of CEPA prohibiting false declarations.

Warning: These elements are based on a time-stamped interview transcript with an expert placed in
the citizen file. These are reported comments and assertions by the author, and not an official
finding made by any court or government agency.

#### f) The municipal component
- Municipal council meetings of July 7 and 14, 2026: The hypothesis of anoxia, first presented as a
-   “scientific certainty” in the municipal press release of April 15, 2026, was reclassified as a
-   simple “hypothesis” during the debates; the City admitted that it had no own analysis results.
- Request for access to information of June 30, 2026: The municipal legal department responded that
-   there was “no document corresponding to what you are looking for” concerning federal reports or
-   independent expertise.
- Public health measures: Refusal to issue a preventive alert or install no-swimming advisories for
-   the summer of 2026 at Lacs Fauvel.
- Allocation of the expropriation fund: Citizen proposal — declined — to allocate the interest from
-   the expropriation fund of $17 million (frozen) to the financing of independent citizen and
-   university environmental monitoring, with the City prioritizing the use of its reserves for the
-   appeal against the Loi 93.

#### g) The Blainville–Stablex agreement of March 11, 2020
The bilateral contractual agreement of March 11, 2020 provided for the allocation of the “Residual
Part” (lot 2,272,801) for the purposes of environmental compensatory measures. The complaint
maintains that the existence of these bilateral contractual commitments has limited the access of
third parties and citizens to local suspensive administrative or civil remedies.

### 5.10 — The legal bases invoked in the annex
In addition to the strict legislative texts, the 102-page analytical annex mobilizes several pillars
of jurisprudence and general principles of Canadian law:
- R. v. Sault Ste. Marie, [1978] 2 R.C.S. 1299: Principle judgment establishing strict liability in
-   environmental regulatory matters (the absence of guilty intent does not exempt from due
-   diligence).
- R. v. Cadillac Fairview Corp. , 2013 ONCJ 94: Confirms that the prohibition in article 32 of LEP
-   fully applies to unintentional harm resulting from industrial or commercial activities.
- Reference re Impact Assessment Act, 2023 SCC 23: Scope of residual federal constitutional
-   jurisdiction over the protection of fisheries, birds and species at risk.
- Imperial affair: Principle according to which the historical polluter remains responsible for its
-   contaminants even after the transfer of land (invoked in the file for the liabilities of Camp
-   Bouchard).
- Sections 97 to 101 of SARA: Penal provisions providing for fines of up to $1,000,000 and
-   imprisonment of up to 5 years for serious offenses.
- Article 6 of SARA & Precautionary Principle: The fundamental purpose of the law requires preventing
-   the extinction of wild species without waiting for absolute scientific certainty to be
-   established.

## Part 06 The decision of August 17, 2026, dissected

### 6.1 — The Secretariat’s action framework
The Secretariat of the CCE first clarified the statutory powers conferred on it by the international
treaty, guaranteeing the neutrality and rigor of its instruction:
- Its role is to identify the provisions constituting environmental laws and to evaluate the
-   eligibility criteria provided for in chapter 24 of the ACEUM.
- “It is not the responsibility of the Secretariat to confirm, refute, or offer an interpretation
-   other than that presented by the Author. »
- It does not render a declaratory judgment on the action of a Party at this preliminary stage, its
-   mandate being to instruct the opening of a factual investigation file.
- He recalls that the SEM process is intended to document the effective application of environmental
-   laws and to shed light on the facts, to enlighten the Trinational Council and the public.
- It examined all the information and substantial additions provided by the revised version of July
-   16, 2026.

### 6.2 — The environmental provisions adopted
This is the most technical part of the decision: the Secretariat has validated a set of major
provisions which constitute the heart of Canadian environmental law.
  · Fisheries Act
  · art. 34.4, 35, 36(3), 36(4) — validated in the revised version, 37, 38 [*]
  · Endangered Species Act
  · art. 32(1) and 33 (already retained on June 3); 58, 73 and 79 — newly retained
  · Migratory Birds Act, 1994
  · art. 5.1(1)
  · Migratory Birds Regulations (2022)
  · art. 5(1) a), b) and c)
  · CEPA (1999)
  · art. 2(1)(a.2) and a.3), 68, 71, 90(1.1), 90(1.2), 90(3), 91
  · LCOM, art. 5
  · Recognized as environmental law, but dismissed because it targets the trade and possession of birds. The Secretariat, however, retains central article 5.1(1) and the Migratory Birds Regulations (art. 5(1)), which directly protect nests and habitats against destruction.
  · LEP, art. 18.1
  · Reference rejected due to a discrepancy in textual numbering, the Secretariat having validated in return all the major operational articles of the LEP (art. 32(1), 33, 58, 73 and 79).
  · LEP: preamble ¶13, art. 10.1, 34, 39(1)(c), 83
  · Qualified as environmental laws, the Secretariat uses them as a contextual interpretative framework to assess the scope of federal conservation obligations.
  · CEPA, art. 64
  · Qualified as a reference definition of “toxic substance”, it is linked to normative articles 68, 71, 90 and 91 which are fully retained.
  · CEPA, art. 5.1(1)
  · Retained as a general interpretative context on sustainable development.
  · CEPA, art. 93 and art. 90(1)
  · The Secretariat distinguishes the discretionary power to regulate from the obligation to apply the texts in force. It specifically retains paragraphs 90(1.1), 90(1.2) and 90(3), which govern the verifiable methods of State intervention.
  · Impact Assessment Act, art. 2; Regulation on concrete activities, art. 2, 56 and 57
  · Recognized as environmental laws and used for interpretative purposes. Section 57 remains a key benchmark for the future: it targets the expansion of a hazardous waste facility located 500 m or less from a natural body of water when the capacity increases by 50% or more.
  · UNDRIP Act (art. 5) & 1982 Constitution (art. 35)
  · The Secretariat recalls that its statutory mandate is limited to federal environmental protection laws only, and does not extend to general constitutional litigation, confirming the primacy of the ecological aspect.
  · CCE Guiding Principles on CET (2017)
  · Considered guiding directions rather than formal laws passed by the Parliament of Canada.
  · ACEUM, art. 24.4
  · The Secretariat recalls that trade disputes fall under Chapter 31 of the agreement. By limiting the instruction to the ecological laws of Chapter 24, the Secretariat refocuses the examination on the most robust evidentiary ground: the protection of species and aquatic ecosystems.

### 6.3 — Criterion (2)(c): The probative force of the documentary corpus
To assess the sufficiency of the supporting information (criterion 24.27(2)(c)), the Secretariat
examined the critical mass of the more than 200 documents submitted by William Guindon. Faced with
the exceptional scale of the file for a citizen initiative, the Secretariat methodically structured
the documents into ten evidentiary categories, concentrating its examination on the nerve center of
the file: fish, migratory birds, threatened species and preservation of habitats.

The Secretariat underlined the probative value of the scientific and field elements included in the
file:
- The Englobe impact study (November 2020), based on inventories documenting in particular the Eastern
-   Whip-poor-will and around ten status bird species.
- Englobe's October 2023 characterization — of which the Secretariat factually notes that no specific
-   wildlife inventory was conducted there, corroborating concerns raised by the complainant about
-   seasonal assessment deficiencies.
- The environmental analysis report of MELCCFP (2025), listing 132 species and major favorable
-   habitats.
- The MELCCFP (2024) request for commitments, which explicitly refers to ECCC's federal tools and
-   responsibilities.
- The WaterShed Monitoring scientific report, with its measurements compared to quality criteria for
-   cadmium, lead, arsenic, cobalt, copper and zinc, identifying a station of particular concern and
-   documenting leaching phenomena during rainy weather.
- Photographic surveys of turbidity and fish mortality near the banks.
- Press revelations (notably Le Devoir, March 19, 2025) indicating that Fisheries and Oceans Canada
-   had requested the submission of a request to examine the impacts on fish habitat.

The decisive legal reasoning: William Guindon based his appeal on the total absence of an
identifiable federal decision, inspection or authorization, attested by his requests for access to
information. The Secretariat has fully validated this logic: in the absence of documentation
emanating from Ottawa, the scientific and provincial elements gathered by the complainant constitute
serious and admissible evidence justifying requiring an official response from Canada.

### 6.4 — Criterion (2)(e): the five approaches
  ▸ Recipients
  ▸ Object
  ▸ Answer
  · June 3, 2026
  · Office of the Minister of the Environment, DFO
  · “Formal Request for Investigation — Alleged Violations of the Migratory Birds Convention Act, 1994 and the Fisheries Act at the Stablex Site, Blainville”
  · Two automatic charges
  · June 10, 2026
  · ECCC, DFO
  · Follow-up: request for a file number and information on the team analyzing
  · Automatic acknowledgment
  · June 16, 2026
  · Stablex, with a copy from the ECCC Enforcement Branch and the DFO Quebec regional office
  · Questions for the company about its compliance measures
  · Decisive piece. The Secretariat writes: “It is clear that, through this email, the ECCC entity responsible for enforcing SARA, CEPA, LP, LCOM and RCOM was notified. »
  · June 19, 2026
  · Officials, ministers, wildlife protection divisions and general boxes of ECCC, DFO and Natural Resources Canada
  · “Official prior notification” setting out the facts and alleged violations
  · Acknowledgments of receipt from several firms
  · June 28, 2026
  · Meeting request + “10 specific factual questions”
  · On June 29, an accused who "on the surface does not seem automated": the emails were "sent to the office of Minister Julie Dabrusin as well as to ECCC program experts", and "any response is at their discretion".

Irrefutable proof of due diligence: The Secretariat emphasizes that the June 16, 2026 mailing
copying the ECCC Enforcement Branch and the DFO regional office, combined with the formal
notifications of June 3, 10 and 19, demonstrates beyond any doubt that the federal authorities
responsible for enforcing the Fisheries Act, SARA, MCO and CEPA were duly informed in writing and
had the necessary time to act.

### 6.5 — Criterion (3)(c): remedies
The Secretariat first recalls its generous case law: this criterion is interpreted “in the broad
sense”; it can be satisfied by relying on a complaint filed by someone else; it is evaluated
according to the standard of reasonableness; he recognizes that it is sometimes impossible to act
against a multitude of offenders, that litigation is not always the right strategy, and that
“certain economic and social factors may hinder private recourse.”

The obstacles invoked by the author: he is a minor and therefore cannot take legal action
independently — “an absolute legal barrier”; his legal guardians are not required to compromise the
family patrimony to deputize for the State; he has no resources of his own to pay lawyers, experts
and costs facing “a developer supported by two multinationals”; and above all, he does not challenge
a decision but “precisely the apparent absence of a decision”, which makes an appeal for judicial
review “difficult to circumscribe”.

But the element which carries the decision is elsewhere: despite all this, the author still acted.
On June 29, 2026, he filed an environmental petition with the Office of the Auditor General of
Canada — a mechanism that, unlike an access to information request, “creates a direct line of
communication between the public and ministers” and requires them to respond. The title of the
petition explicitly targeted the “application of environmental laws to the Stablex site in
Blainville: Act respecting fisheries, aquatic habitats, migratory birds and species likely to be
concerned”. The Secretariat concludes that, in circumstances making traditional legal recourse
difficult, this approach constitutes “an adequate means” of satisfying the criterion.

### 6.6 — The device
“For the above-mentioned reasons, the Secretariat determines that communication SEM-26-003 (Landfill
of hazardous materials in Blainville) meets all the eligibility criteria provided for in article
24.27(1), (2) and (3) of ACEUM […] and merits a response from the Party. »

Pursuant to Article 24.27(4), Canada may respond within 60 days, or before October 16, 2026. The
Secretariat will make the response public. If Canada includes confidential information, it must
clearly identify it and justify its basis. Explicit warning: “In the absence of a clear, specific
and substantiated designation […] the Secretariat will not be able to infer the confidentiality of
the information and will treat the information in accordance with the principle of transparency. »

## Part 07 Key benchmarks and legal scope of the procedure

## Part 08 The rest

Canada unveils inspections, technical opinions or the famous examination of impacts on fish habitat
mentioned by Le Devoir. Even if the Secretariat then believed that it did not need a factual file,
the approach would have already achieved a major objective: forcing Ottawa to make public previously
unobtainable documents and to take an official position on the Grande Tourbière.

If the federal response confirms that no inspection or authorization took place, or attempts to hide
behind Quebec authorizations, the Secretariat will recommend the creation of a factual file within
60 days. Since the vote in the Trinational Council is taken by a majority (2 votes out of 3 between
Canada, the United States and Mexico), the Canadian government has no right of veto to block the
investigation.

To stop the process, Canada would have to prove that a federal judicial or administrative proceeding
involving exactly the same omissions is currently underway. However, it is precisely because Ottawa
has not opened any procedure that the complaint was filed: invoking such an exception would
paradoxically force Canada to prove that it is finally taking action.

### Dates to watch out for
  · Friday October 16, 2026
  · Canada's response filed (Exhibit 72-RSP — 59 p.): Ottawa requests procedural closure invoking the municipal judicial review of Blainville and federal petition no. 0546.
  · Saturday October 17, 2026
  · Citizen's reply filed (Memorandum of observations): William Guindon demonstrates the inapplicability of art. 24.27(4)(a) (no identity of subject with the municipal dispute) and requires the creation of a factual record.
  · Tuesday November 10, 2026
  · Legal deadline BVG No. 0546: Mandatory deadline for the response of federal ministers to environmental petition No. 0546 filed with the Office of the Auditor General (art. 22 LVG).
  · November 2026
  · Parliamentary meetings in Ottawa: Follow-up meetings confirmed in Parliament with Nadine Le Gal (Office of the leader of the Bloc Québécois) and Senator Madeleine Chenette (Senate of Canada).
  · January 2027 (60 working days)
  · End of the 60 working day period (art. 24.28(1) ACEUM, exhibit 73-REC) available to the CCE Secretariat to recommend to the Ministerial Council the creation of a factual file.
  · 2027
  · If the Council gives the green light (2/3 vote): 120 days of drafting, 30 days of comments from the Parties, then official publication of the international investigation report.

## Part 09 Methodological benchmarks and reading of technical data

### 9.1 — Cartographic precision: four distinct reference perimeters
Public documents relating to the Stablex project mention four different areas which do not
contradict each other, but each correspond to a specific scope of analysis:
- 9 ha of wetlands and 58 ha of woodlands: areas attached to the lot expropriated by the Loi 93, cited
-   by the BAPE and the media.
- 29.2 ha of wetlands (56% of a 52 ha study area): biophysical perimeter assessed in the Englobe
-   impact study.
- 27.8 ha (278,000 m²): direct footprint of the planned backfill for cell no. 6.
- 13 ha: wetland clearing area covered in sectoral authorizations.

### 9.2 — Hydrochemical complementarity: surface water (320×) and sediments (31.8× to 57.6×)
The evidentiary corpus documents two series of cadmium measurements which clarify each other
hydrologically:
- Citizen sampling on December 2, 2023 (surface water) revealed an acute peak of 0.016 mg/L, or 320
-   times the criterion for protecting aquatic life.
- The WaterShed Monitoring accredited scientific campaign (October-November 2025) (ICP-MS analyses)
-   measured an exceedance of 4.7 times the threshold in the water column, and a concentration
-   reaching 31.8 to 57.6 times the rare endpoint (CER) in sediment.

As the communication demonstrates, these two matrices reflect two complementary dynamics: the
concentration in surface water varies rapidly depending on episodes of rain and runoff, while
sediments act as a geochemical memory accumulating heavy metals over time.

### 9.3 — Waste of American origin: three economic and physical indicators
The proportions associated with American inputs in the file refer to three distinct units of
measurement:
- Nearly 50% of revenues: share of turnover mentioned by the company's management in 2025.
- Nearly 30% of buried volumes: historical proportion documented during the BAPE work.
- 17% of imported volumes (2024): annual data cited in media coverage.

### 9.4 — Citation benchmarks in the official determination (62/DET)
For researchers and jurists wishing to cite directly the official PDF of the decision of August 17,
2026 (reference A24.27(2)(3)/SEM/26-003/62/DET), three particularities of the numbering of the
original document should be noted:
- The discussion of private remedies (section 24.27(3)(c)) appears under the section heading
-   "24.27(3)(b)", although the cited text and conclusion in paragraph 79 specifically refer to
-   paragraph (3)(c).
- Consideration of prior written communication to authorities (article 24.27(2)(e)) is dealt with in
-   paragraphs 71 to 73.
- In the final operative part (paragraph 80), a typographical bullet jump goes from (iii) to (v): to
-   cite the formal validation of sections 34.4, 35, 36(3), 36(4), 37 and 38 of the Fisheries Act, it
-   is appropriate to refer directly to paragraphs 29 to 33 of the determination.

### 9.5 — Filing and registration dates
The initial communication bears the date of signature and electronic filing of May 1, 2026 (retained
in the press releases) and the date of administrative registration in the Secretariat register of
May 4, 2026 (working day serving as the starting point for calculating procedural deadlines).

## Part 10 The Questions Everyone Asks

Can a minor really file an international complaint? Yes. The treaty speaks of “any person of a
Party”: no age requirement. A tasty paradox: he cannot go before a court in his own country without
legal representation, but he can refer the matter to a trinational commission. Moreover, it is
precisely this internal impossibility which allowed it to satisfy the criterion of private remedies.

Why target Ottawa and not Quebec, when it is Quebec that authorized everything? Two reasons. First,
the legal: the SEM mechanism is only aimed at central governments — Quebec literally cannot be
targeted. Then, the strategic: the Loi 93 closed the provincial appeals, and two courts confirmed it
in April 2025. Only the federal one remained.

How can a federal law apply to private land in Quebec? By fish and birds. Fish and their habitat, as
well as migratory birds, fall under federal jurisdiction across the country, regardless of who owns
the land. Section 36(3) of the Fisheries Act prohibits the discharge of a deleterious substance into
waters frequented by fish “or to any other place” from which it may arrive — a ditch, for example.
This is what makes this article so powerful.

If the factual record is published, what is its real impact? Its institutional, scientific and
political impact is major. The factual record is an independent international investigation led by
the three governments of North America. It allows neutral experts to be commissioned, the law
enforcement practices of federal departments to be examined, and the public factual and scientific
truth to be set in stone. In several historical precedents (such as the SEM-97-005 or North Atlantic
Right Whale affair), these reports have forced administrative reforms, fueled parliamentary work and
served as key evidence in the public space.

Has this mechanism already worked? He produced published factual files, including against Canada.
The most famous in this context is SEM-97-005, on acid discharges in the St. Lawrence: the
investigators appointed by the Secretariat concluded that Environment Canada had modified an
inspection report to the point that the signing official declared that he no longer recognized it.
The Secretariat has also just published, in December 2025, a factual file on the North Atlantic
right whale (SEM-21-003), and in August 2025 another on the Gulf of California porpoise.

How long can the procedure last given the progress of the work? Submitted in May 2026, the process
is moving through its stages at a sustained pace: following the submission of Canada's response
(72-RSP) on October 16, 2026 and the citizen's reply of October 17, 2026, the Secretariat has 60
working days (art. 24.28(1) ACEUM) to recommend a factual file, while federal ministers are legally
required to respond to BVG Petition No. 0546 by November 10, 2026. If the construction machinery
operates in the short term, the SEM mechanism establishes federal responsibility over time: each
destruction of habitat or release of contaminants occurring during Ottawa's inaction increases the
factual toll for which the government will have to answer to its ACEUM partners.

What is the value and scope of the file filed by William Guindon? The legal result is exceptional:
while the vast majority of citizen communications are rejected at the preliminary stage of
admissibility by the Secretariat of the CCE, that of William Guindon successfully passed all the
admissibility criteria of articles 24.27(1), (2) and (3) of the ACEUM. The Secretariat has validated
the probative force of its corpus of 222 documentary pieces (university hydrogeological studies,
wildlife characterizations, accredited laboratory analyzes of cadmium exceedances, ministerial
inspection records). It is a tour de force all the more remarkable because it was accomplished in a
completely autonomous and voluntary manner by a 15-year-old citizen, reaching where no municipality
or any Quebec legal action had succeeded in forcing the State to be publicly accountable.

## Part 11 Complete Glossary

- ACEUM

Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement, in force since July 1, 2020. Replaces NAFTA. Its chapter 24
focuses on the environment. Called USMCA in English, T-MEC in Spanish.
- ACE

Environmental cooperation agreement, signed in parallel with the ACEUM. It is he who organizes the
CCE itself.
- Anoxia

Absence of oxygen in the water. Common cause of fish mortality — but, as the case points out, it is
the mechanism of death, not its origin.
- BAPE

Bureau d'audiences publiques sur l'environnement (Québec Bureau of Public Environmental Hearings),
Quebec organization which holds hearings and provides opinions to the government. Its report No. 371
focused on this project and recommended against authorization.
- Gag

Exceptional parliamentary procedure which limits the time of debate to force the rapid adoption of a
bill. Used for Loi 93 March 27-28, 2025.
- CCE

Commission de coopération environnementale. Trinational organization created in 1994, based in
Montreal. Three bodies: Council, Secretariat, Joint Public Advisory Committee.
- Cell (burial)

Waterproof compartment dug into the ground, isolated by a membrane, where waste is placed. Stablex
operates five; the sixth is the subject of the dispute.
- CER (rare endpoint)

Reference threshold for sediments: level below which virtually no toxic effects on bottom-dwelling
organisms are expected.
- CVAC (chronic aquatic life criterion)

Reference threshold for water: concentration beyond which prolonged exposure harms aquatic
organisms.
- Hexavalent chromium

Particularly toxic form of chromium, recognized carcinogen by inhalation. Not to be confused with
trivalent chromium, which is much less dangerous.
- Communication / Observation

The two names for the same thing: the citizen complaint. The treaty says “observations”, the
Commission says “communications”.
- Advice (from CCE)

The environment ministers of the three countries, or their representatives. This is the body that
votes on the creation of a factual file — by 2 votes out of 3.
- Decree 515-2025

Quebec decree authorizing the project and setting its conditions. The provincial notices of
non-compliance mentioned in the file relate to compliance with these conditions.
- Determination

The written and reasoned decision of the Secretariat at each stage of filtering. This is not a
judgment.
- Factual record

The possible end product of the process: a public investigation report bringing together technical,
scientific and legal data, interviews with officials and analyzes from independent experts. He
states the facts without concluding, for the public to judge.
- ECCC

Environment and Climate Change Canada. Its Enforcement Branch is the agency responsible for
enforcing federal environmental laws.
- Take legal action

Legal expression meaning “to be able to act before a court”. A minor cannot go to court alone.
- Critical habitat

SARA concept: habitat necessary for the survival or recovery of a listed species. Its destruction is
prohibited by article 58.
- Hibernacle

Underground shelter where reptiles spend the winter. Central argument of the case: the snakes are
inaccessible there before May, therefore invisible during deforestation ending on April 15.
- ICP-MS

Inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometry: reference laboratory technique for measuring metals at
very low concentrations. This is the method used by the WaterShed campaign.
- NPRI

National inventory of pollutant releases: federal register where companies are required to declare
their releases. An inaccurate statement is subject to prosecution under CEPA.
- LCOM / RCOM

Migratory Birds Convention Act, 1994 and Regulations (2022 version). Sometimes abbreviated ROM in
the complaint.
- CEPA

Canadian Environmental Protection Act (1999), modernized in 2023 to include the right to a healthy
environment.
- THE P

Endangered Species Act (2002). Its appendix 1 lists protected species, classified from “concerning”
to “extinct”.
- Loi 93

Quebec law (L.Q. 2025, c. 7), official title: Act concerning in particular the transfer of ownership
of an immovable in the Ville de Blainville. Adopted under gag order on March 28, 2025 by 61 votes to
31. It expropriates the City for $17 million and authorizes “no appeal for judicial review […]
except on a question of jurisdiction”.
- MELCCFP (Québec Ministry of the Environment, the Fight against Climate Change, Wildlife and Parks)

Ministère de l'Environnement, de la Lutte contre les changements climatiques, de la Faune et des
Parcs (Québec Ministry of the Environment, the Fight against Climate Change, Wildlife and Parks)
from Quebec.
- MY

Suspended solids: solid particles floating in water. They make the water cloudy, clog the gills and
smother the spawning grounds.
- DFO

Fisheries and Oceans Canada, responsible for enforcing the Fisheries Act.
- “Failing to apply effectively”

The key formula of the entire chapter 24. It does not aim at the quality of the laws, but at
enforcing them in practice: inspect, investigate, authorize, sanction.
- Environmental petition

Canadian federal mechanism administered by the Office of the Auditor General: anyone residing in
Canada can raise environmental concerns with federal ministers, who are required to respond. It is
this mechanism which unlocked the criterion of private remedies.
- Prima facie

Latin expression: “at first sight”. Prima facie evidence is sufficient to warrant review, not to
establish guilt.
- S.E.M.

English acronym for Submissions on Enforcement Matters. Refers to the entire citizen complaint
process of articles 24.27 and 24.28.
- Sphagnum

Moss characteristic of peat bogs, which retains water like a sponge and acidifies the environment.
Its mounds provide shelter for the four-toed salamander.
- Bog

Humid environment where organic matter accumulates without completely decomposing due to lack of
oxygen. Filters water, stores huge amounts of carbon, is home to specialized wildlife. Once
destroyed, it cannot be reconstituted on the scale of a human life.
- Turbidity

Measurement of water turbidity. High turbidity after rain, downstream of a construction site,
indicates sediment leaching.
- Two-row wampum

Ancestral treaty between the Haudenosaunee nations and Europeans, materialized by a belt of pearls
with two parallel rows: two boats navigating on the same river without one directing the other.
Invoked in the complaint, but excluded from the legal analysis.

evolution between the procedural acts and the way in which the documents were analyzed and
consolidated by the Secretariat.

### 12.2 — Methodical instruction and categorization by the Secretariat
Faced with the exceptional richness of the evidentiary corpus submitted by William Guindon - more
than 200 scientific, technical, cartographic and legal documents - the Secretariat of the CCE
proceeded to a methodical structuring of the file into ten major categories of evidentiary analysis:
- Impact studies and ecological characterizations: Integration of Englobe reports (2020 and 2023) and
-   Quebec government inventories documenting protected species and their habitats.
- Independent hydrochemical campaigns: Analysis of WaterShed Monitoring data highlighting excesses in
-   heavy metals (notably cadmium, arsenic, lead).
- Ministerial inspections and compliance: Taking into account provincial inspection reports noting
-   encroachment on the riparian strip and vegetation management.
- Failure to issue federal authorizations: Exploitation of requests for access to information
-   demonstrating the inaction of the Canadian federal apparatus.
- Field verifications and visual documentation: Photographic records documenting episodes of aquatic
-   mortality and sediment transfers during rainy weather.

This rigorous educational approach made it possible to validate the admissibility of the
communication on all of the fundamental laws, establishing a historical precedent for a citizen
initiative led by a 15-year-old author.

PAGE 9/50 · https://williamguindon.me/autochtone.html
Title : Aboriginal History & Blainville Bog · William Guindon
==============================================================================

## A broad file, not a legend.

The broadest portrait that open sources allow us to write in 2026: the people, the peat bog, the
surrounding water territories, the species. The thickness is over time. Nothing is invented to fill
a silence.

A civic and rigorous approach to documentary research. Each fact is linked to a verifiable source:
public archives, ministries, BAPE (Québec Bureau of Public Environmental Hearings), journalistic
investigations, ecological studies and direct transmissions. When data diverge, the contradiction is
exposed with transparency and honesty.

It is neither a speaking out on behalf of the First Nations, nor an impersonal synthesis produced on
an assembly line by algorithms. This document does not replace either the living memory of the
elders, nor the sovereign teachings of the Clan Mothers of the Longhouse. Colonial archives have
often obscured the indigenous presence on these lands; this work seeks to name this silence with
humility, without ever inventing anything.

## A flat country, between river and mountain.

Blainville is a city in the Lower Laurentians, on the north shore of Montreal, in the MRC (regional
county municipality) de Thérèse-De Blainville (Thérèse-De Blainville regional county municipality)
and the Metropolitan Community of Montreal. It is located around 45°40′ north latitude and 73°53′
west longitude. Area: approximately 55 km². Population: 59,819 inhabitants at the 2021 census;
61,114 in 2024, according to the current encyclopedic record. Kind: Blainvillois, Blainvilloise.
Mayor in 2026: Liza Poulin.

It’s not yet the mountain. This is the plain of the St. Lawrence Lowlands: marine clays, almost zero
slope, water that does not find its slope. The Rivière aux Chiens, a tributary of the Rivière des
Mille Îles, borders the south of the city. To the north and east, the wooded area continues towards
Sainte-Anne-des-Plaines. To the west: Boisbriand, Sainte-Thérèse, then Saint-Eustache, Lac des Deux
Montagnes, Oka and Kanehsatà:ke. To the south, on the other side of the river: Laval, Île Jésus.

Source: Environment page of the Ville de Blainville (City of Blainville). Woodlands and wetlands
partly overlap: a wooded peat bog can count in both.

The bog is not a point on a map. It is a complex: peat bogs, marshes, wooded and shrub swamps, wet
meadows, connected to streams which end in the river. The ministry describes a group of
approximately 622.7 hectares, included in the Atlas of territories of interest for conservation in
the St. Lawrence Lowlands.

#### Fauvel Lakes
Public woodland of 97 ha, inaugurated in 2017. Marshes, wooded peat bogs, two bodies of water, trail
of approximately 3.5 km. More than 100 species of birds announced by the City and its partners.

#### Bouchard map
Largely swampy woodland, footbridges, remains of a WWII munitions filling factory. The place where
industrial warfare bit into the damp country.

#### Trail to Sainte-Anne
A cycle path crosses the area and connects Blainville to Sainte-Anne-des-Plaines. It is often
through this that people discover the immensity of the peat bog – and, further on, the scars.

## Before names, there was a sea.

The bog was not “always there”. It is the last state of a hollow left by the ice, then by the sea,
then by a lake.

#### Champlain Sea
At the end of the last glaciation, the Laurentide ice sheet retreated. The ground, still buried
under the weight of the ice, allows the sea to enter. The Champlain Sea covers the Lowlands. The
syntheses of Occhietti and Richard place this episode around 13,000 to 10,500 years before today, in
calendar years, with variations depending on the sites and calibration. The entire Blainville plain
was then under salt water. Whales once swam where bungalows grow today. During this interval, no
human occupation is possible here: a seabed is not a camp.

#### Lake, then foam
The land rises, the sea recedes, and lakes remain trapped in marine clay, impermeable, sometimes
sensitive: the clay of the Champlain Sea. The City and the ministry write that the large peat bogs
in the Blainville woods could date back almost 6,000 years, and that the land targeted by Stablex is
surrounded by large peat bogs “which could date back 6,000 years”. The precise carbon-14 dating of
this specific complex remains to be documented by future university samples. This is an official
estimate, consistent with the age of many lowland peatlands, which begins in the mid-Holocene, once
the depressions were isolated.

#### How a bog is built
Sphagnum moss grows from the top and dies from the bottom. In cold, acidic, oxygen-poor water,
decomposition slows down. Peat piles up from a few tenths of a millimeter to a few millimeters per
year. In six millennia, this can create several meters of archive. An ombrotrophic peat bog ends up
receiving only rainwater. A fen, or fen, remains nourished by soil water, richer in minerals. The
Blainville complex combines the two, with marshes and swamps. Le Devoir, citing the documents in the
file, describes an ecosystem of approximately 6.2 km²: marshes, wooded and shrub swamps, wet meadows
and peat bogs.

The ministry writes that, in addition to habitat, hydrological regulation, sediment capture,
screening against wind and sun, and the landscape, “one of the important ecological functions to
preserve is that of a carbon sink”. He adds that the planned activities threaten the sustainability
of adjacent wetlands: hydrology, plant and microbial communities, water chemistry, soil quality. A
specialist cited by Le Devoir recalls that visible degradation is very difficult to reverse, and
that the disappearance of the complex could release the already sequestered carbon. The exact
quantitative assessment of the tonnage of carbon stored in this complex is part of the independent
environmental studies still to be investigated.

## Several nations, the same country of water.

Two errors respond to each other. Assign the bog to a single people. Or start the story in 1683,
with a lordship. A third would be to invent an oral story that no public source contains.

### What “unassigned” means here
For the island of Montreal and the south of the Laurentians, there is no numbered treaty by which a
nation ceded its title to the Crown, as in part of the West. The French lordships were granted
without the consent that contemporary law would recognize. Kanien’kehá:ka traditional authorities
maintain that their traditional territory was never ceded. The press, for the Kahnawà:ke nation,
describes it as extending from the St. Lawrence Valley to the Adirondacks and northern New York
State, including the Montreal region. McGill University, in its territorial recognition, names the
Kanien’kehá:ka guardians of the lands and waters of Tiohtià:ke. An educational document from UQTR
places the south of the Laurentians in the Kanien’kehá:ka ancestral territory, and the Weskarini, an
Anishinabe group, further north, in the Hautes-Laurentides.

No court judgment says: “the peat bog lot belongs to such and such a community”. The fact,
historical and political, is simpler. This country was not ceded by treaty. Several nations have
attachments to it that overlap in time. Making it an exclusive queue would be wrong.

#### Kanien’kehá:ka
Autonym: the people of flint. In Kanien'kéha, the traditional longhouse people are the
Kanonsonnkweonkwe (or Kannosonhkwe'ón:we; Rotinonhsón:ni), distinct from the Onondaga term
Haudenosaunee. Guardians of the Eastern Gate. Nearby communities: Kanehsatà:ke (Oka), Kahnawà:ke
(south of Montreal), Akwesasne. Tioweró:ton hunting territory, in the Laurentians, shared between
Kahnawà:ke and Kanehsatà:ke. The southern Laurentians are described as part of their ancestral
territory.

#### Anishinabeg, Nipissing, Abenaki
The Algonquins frequented Lac des Deux Montagnes and the Ottawa Valley. In 1674, Father Dalmas noted
an “Algonquin cabin” on Île Jésus. The Nipissing joined the Oka mission in 1727. The Abenakis named
the Mille Îles River Makigôtekw, the river of rakes.

#### St. Lawrence Iroquoians
Corn, squash and bean farmers, palisaded villages, including Hochelaga visited by Cartier in 1535.
Their sites in the valley disappeared between Cartier and Champlain (1603). Open debate: war,
epidemics, climate, adoption by the Wendat, the Haudenosaunee, the Algonquins, the Abenakis. They
did not “disappear without leaving anyone behind”; they ceased to exist as a distinct nation on the
river.

#### Wendat
Historical allies of the French, adversaries and then parents of the Haudenosaunee. Certain Wendat
traditions, and the work of archaeologists, link some of the dispersed St. Lawrence Iroquoians to
Wendat communities. Their direct presence on the Blainville peat bog is not documented. Their
history sheds light on the region. She doesn't own it alone.

A major political and legal distinction separates the Longhouse (traditional sovereign governance,
carried by the Clan Mothers, hereditary chiefs and the Great Law of Peace / Kaianerehkó:wa) from the
system of band councils imposed by the British Crown and the Indian Act to administer the colonial
regime. Bodies such as band councils or the Assembly of First Nations (AFN) are state creations
which in no way replace the inherent sovereignty of the peoples of the Long House
(Kanonsonnkweonkwe). To understand the political significance of this terminological and
institutional distinction, see the webinar on traditional sovereignty and Kannosonhkwe'ón:we ↗.

### Pronounce
  ▸ Name
  ▸ Approximate meaning
  ▸ Landmark
  · Kanien’kehá:ka

  · the sources diverge, the two traditions circulate

These pronunciations are benchmarks for a French-speaking ear, not a linguistic transcription. The
spelling varies: Kanien’kehá:ka, Kanien’kéha, Kanehsatà:ke, Kanesatake, Kannosonhkwe’ón:we.

### An indigenous timeline of the country around
Paleoindian groups, hunters, entered southern Quebec as the ice retreated. The Blainville plain is
still marine, or has just emerged. No camp can be placed there without proof.

The Archaic intensifies in the valley: hunting, fishing, gathering, cut stone and polished stone.
This is also the order of magnitude used for the start of the peat bog. Human history and plant
history become contemporary, without a published archaeological site linking them to this precise
location.

Artifacts associated with the Mille Îles River, unearthed during excavations mentioned for 1996, are
estimated between 3000 BCE and the year 1000. The source is secondary: a general public synthesis
based on the archeology of the river. The interval covers the Late Archaic and the Woodland:
pottery, points, bank camps. The river is a road long before being a lordship.

In the valley, Iroquoian groups settled around corn. Hochelaga, on the island of Montreal, was a
stockaded village of around a thousand people when Cartier passed there in 1535. The exact site
remains debated. An open bog is not a village location: too wet, too acidic for corn. This is the
hinterland. Hunting, berries, plants, water, refuge.

The Iroquoian villages of the Saint Lawrence cease. Champlain, in 1603 and then in 1611, no longer
found Hochelaga. In European sources, the river once again becomes a corridor of passage, more than
a country of agricultural villages.

The Jesuit Relations describe Île Jésus, then Île Montmagny, “beautiful and large”, bordered to the
north by the Saint-Jean River – one of the ancient names of the Mille Îles River, associated with
Jean Nicolet.

Father Antoine Dalmas tours Jesus Island. He finds two indigenous camps. One is noted as an
“Algonquin cabin”. The island serves as a stopover, especially in the summer, for the fur trade.
Artifacts from these passages are still reported.

The colonization of Jesus Island stalled, partly because of Iroquois opposition. On September 24,
1683, according to the municipal timeline, Michel-Sidrac Dugué de Boisbriand received the lordship
of Mille-Îles, from Terrebonne to the Rivière du Chêne, in Saint-Eustache. The Canadian Encyclopedia
indicates 1673. Both dates are in circulation. The City retains 1683. A concession is not proof of
an empty country.

The Sault-au-Récollet mission is moved to Lac des Deux Montagnes. In 1721, villages opened:
Kanien'kehá:ka on one side, Algonquins and soon Nipissing on the other, under the Sulpicians. The
king grants a lordship on the condition, according to several readings, that the lands are used by
the Aboriginal people. The Sulpicians will then claim ownership. The conflict is not peripheral. It
is the origin of Kanehsatà:ke, about thirty kilometers west of the peat bog. It will break out in
1990.

Algonquin converts from the mission often spent the winter on their hunting grounds in the Ottawa
Valley, and the summer near the lake. The Kanien’kehá:ka of the mission remain more attached to the
village. The south of the Laurentians is a country crossed, hunted, claimed. It is not a blank page
between two clearings.

Patriots from Saint-Eustache, led in particular by Amury Girod, marched towards Oka and Kanehsatà:ke
to look for weapons and “disarm the savages”, according to judicial sources of the time. The same
fall, a patriot expedition to Kahnawà:ke failed: the Kanien’kehá:ka captured the attackers and
delivered them to Montreal. The school history of the Patriots often omits this chapter. However, it
takes place on the same river.

Battle of Saint-Eustache. Colborne attempts to cross the Mille Îles River. Nearly 70 Patriots were
killed. The village is pillaged. The river is a battle line, not a setting.

Creation of the Doncaster reserve, Tioweró:ton, hunting and fishing territory in the Laurentians,
for the use of the Mohawks of Kahnawà:ke and Kanesatake. It's not the bog. This is proof that the
relationship with the Laurentian Forest did not end with the seigneurial regime.

Oka crisis, or Kanehsatà:ke resistance. A golf course and a residential project threaten a pine
forest which includes a cemetery. Seventy-eight days. A police officer killed. The army. The Mercier
Bridge blocked by Kahnawà:ke. The conflict comes from a title that has never been settled since
1717. Blainville is not Oka. It’s the same basin, and the same story: wet and wooded lands treated
as empty, because a European land register saw neither the deaths nor the uses.

## Understanding colonial archives and honoring indigenous memory.

Conventional municipal stories traditionally begin with the seigneurial concession of 1683,
Marie-Thérèse de Blainville or the Bouchard Plan, retracing an urban history of more than 300 years.
However, the plant landscape and the memory of the territory extend over several millennia.

Three essential factors enable us to understand the nature of written archives:

1. A lifestyle that respects the environment. Wetlands and peatlands were used for hunting, seasonal
gathering (small fruits, medicinal plants) and land or river routes. This respectful relationship
with the land left a light mark on the landscape, without the construction of permanent sedentary
structures in the swamp.

2. The prism of colonial archives. European chroniclers and missionaries mainly recorded the
establishment of parishes, censives and trading posts, often perceiving wetlands as obstacles rather
than nurturing ecosystems.

3. Future archaeological projects. The banks of the Rivière aux Chiens and the surroundings of the
Mille Îles basin constitute areas of potential archaeological interest whose in-depth study must be
considered in close consultation with the nations holding these lands.

The margins of the peat bog — drier cords, terraces of the Rivière aux Chiens, approaches to the
Rivière des Mille Îles — represent major ecological transition zones. The heart of the peat complex
constitutes a valuable paleoenvironmental archive: ancient pollens, sediments and traces of past
ecosystems. The detailed analysis of pollen cores in Blainville represents an open area of
​​scientific research to better understand the plant and climatic history of the Lower Laurentians.

### Plants of the humid country, ancestral knowledge of the North-East
This table presents the ethnobotanical procession traditionally documented within several nations of
the North-East for the species characteristic of these humid habitats:
  ▸ Plant
  ▸ Medium
  ▸ Documented uses, on a national scale
  · Labrador tea Rhododendron groenlandicum
  · peat bogs, acidic undergrowth
  · Very widespread infusion. Leaves used against inflammation in many indigenous pharmacopoeias. Dyeing. Reported by local naturalist observers.
  · Cranberry Vaccinium oxycoccos and relatives
  · sphagnum carpet
  · Autumn fruit, eaten fresh, dried or integrated into pemmican. An emblematic food plant of peat bogs whose local presence is characteristic of this habitat.
  · Sphagnum Sphagnum
  · the very body of the bog
  · Several Northeastern nations used them for their natural absorbent and antiseptic properties. They constitute the living matter of the peat bog.
  · Cattail Typha
  · marshes, water edges
  · Food rhizome, pollen, leaves for mats and basketry. Plant present in marshes and minerotrophic borders.
  · Thuja / cedar Thuja occidentalis
  · minerotrophic swamps
  · Tree of great ceremonial, medicinal and purifying importance among the Haudenosaunee and Anishinabeg. Present in adjacent wet cedar groves.
  · Willows Salix
  · wet edges
  · Bark rich in salicin, traditionally used for its soothing and pain-relieving properties. Basketwork material.
  · Wild garlic Allium tricoccum
  · nearby rich maple groves
