## Makigôtekw, the road that holds the whole country together.

The Mille Îles River is an outlet from Lac des Deux Montagnes, therefore from the Outaouais, to the
Rivière des Prairies. Around 42 kilometers, a drop of around fifteen meters, around a hundred
islands covering around 350 hectares. It forms the northern limit of Île Jésus. Without it, the peat
bog is just a basin. With it, the Blainville wetland complex belongs to a living corridor, from Oka
to Terrebonne.

In European sources, it was called the Saint-Jean River from the Relation of 1637, then the Jesus
River, the La Chesnaye River, the Repentigny River, and Branch of the River Ottawa. The French name
comes from “Mil Isles”, the archipelago between the lake and Terrebonne, fixed as the name of a
watercourse in the 18th century. Boucault wrote “Milse Isle” in 1754. The Abenaki name, Makigôtekw,
rakes river, is older than the seigneury. The "rakes" have been read as fisheries, or as the comb
shape of channels between islands. Both readings remain glosses.

#### Wildlife refuge, 1998
Ten islands, 26.2 ha, in Laval and Rosemère, managed with Éco-Nature. Regulation which protects in
particular the habitat of the map turtle, the brown snake, the wood duck and the Canada goose.

#### 92 status species
The refuge and its partners target, among others, the copper redhorse, the river redhorse, the
eastern sand darter, the channel darter, the grass shiner, the American water-willow, the least
bittern, the Canada warbler, turtles, bats, and the monarch.

#### A fish that only exists here
The copper redhorse, Moxostoma hubbsi, is endemic to southwestern Quebec, endangered. Its critical
habitat includes portions of the Mille Îles and Des Prairies rivers. He does not live in the bog. It
lives in the river that drains it. Cutting one means impoverishing the other.

In 2016, La Revue de Terrebonne recalled an often cited figure: 99.9% of the Canadian population of
American carmantine, a bank plant, is found along this watercourse. This is a widely publicized
conservation fact. Before legal use, it remains to be re-verified in the official CDPNQ records.

Éco-Nature suggests that the Mille Îles River is home to more animal and plant species than
Mont-Tremblant National Park. Mediation formula, not a statistical table reproduced here. The
intuition holds: the wealth is in the islands, the seagrass beds and the riverside marshes, not in
the altitude.

## The soft soil protected the bog. A time.

The seigneury of Mille-Îles encompasses what will become Saint-Eustache, Bois-des-Filion, Lorraine,
Rosemère, Boisbriand, Sainte-Thérèse and Blainville. The City writes that it was necessary to wait
until the second half of the 18th century for the territory of Blainville to open up, very
gradually, under the leadership of Marie-Thérèse de Blainville, daughter of Louis-Jean-Baptiste
Céloron de Blainville, and her husband Jean-Marie Nolan Lamarque. The first inhabitant often cited,
along the river, is Joseph Phillion, whose name moved towards Bois-des-Filion.

Why so late, when Montreal was founded in 1642? Because it’s not easy wheat growing land. Forest,
clay, stagnant water, distance and, in the 17th century, military insecurity. Peatlands are the last
plots that agriculture desires. You don't plow a carpet of sphagnum moss. We bypass it, we drain it
if we can, we despise it.

Disagreement between Lord Hertel and Lordship Lamarque. The seigneury is split by the Grande Ligne,
today Boulevard du Curé-Labelle, route 117. The Canadian Encyclopedia dates the episode to 1791 and
speaks of a mill conflict. The north-south axis of the city comes from this dispute, not from an old
published path.

Sainte-Thérèse became a municipality in 1849. The surrounding area was established as the parish of
Sainte-Thérèse-de-Blainville. The modern municipal regime is established on a seigneurial land
register.

Detachments: Boisbriand, Rosemère, Bois-des-Filion. Blainville is what remains, then what refuses to
merge with Sainte-Thérèse.

City status, under the Boisvert administration. The municipal page remembers June 29. Secondary
notices say June 14. Eight mayors since: Roger Boisvert, André De Carufel, Paul Mercier, Oneil
Charron, Pierre Gingras, François Cantin, Richard Perreault, Liza Poulin.

The name of the city does not come from an indigenous toponym. It comes from an officer,
Jean-Baptiste Céloron de Blainville. Renaming does not erase the previous country. This only makes
it unreadable on signs.

## What we do with land deemed worthless.

The big turning point was not 1968. It was 1941. The federal government expropriated, in a few
weeks, a huge piece of land in the parish to set up Plan Bouchard, a munitions filling complex. It
is not an explosives factory in the strict sense, but an assembly workshop: tracer bullets, shells,
bombs, mines, depth charges, torpedoes. Local sources speak of approximately 6,000 acres, some 339
buildings, and 6,000 to 8,000 workers. The Canadian Encyclopedia records 1939 and 6,000 employees.
The numbers vary. The scale, no. A temporary industrial town grows in the damp woodland.

The war ended in 1945. The complex became Camp Bouchard, a depot and training camp, until around
1969 according to the encyclopedia. In 1981, the City acquired other federal acres and sold part of
them to Stablex. Local heritage notices speak of 12 acres. The military site was decontaminated
around 1995. This does not resolve the chemical story that begins next door.

#### Stablex, since 1983
A subsidiary linked to an American group, Stablex processes inorganic residual hazardous materials
by stabilization and solidification, the Sealosafe process. The residues are mixed with cementitious
binders, then buried. Five historic cells. Cell 6, planned on neighboring municipal land, would
target more than eight million tonnes over around forty years, a horizon sometimes announced until
2067.

#### The bog as trash
Part of the complex was used as a dump. In October 2014, a citizen and municipal cleanup removed
more than 61,000 liters of waste and seven hunting caches. Nest boxes are placed for the tree
swallow, the northern flicker and the downy woodpecker. The tires and the ocher pools along the
track have unwillingly become the landscape through which many discover the place.

There is a continuity, moral as well as administrative, between the seigneurial judgment (“useless
for the plow”), the military judgment (“perfect for explosives, no one lives there”) and the
industrial judgment (“site already disturbed, low value”). The BAPE and the studies commissioned by
the company itself have cracked this latest judgment. They did not prevent it from becoming law.

## The species, with their degree of proof.

Each sheet carries its degree of proof. A magnificent plant cited in a petition does not have the
weight of a fish counted in a report submitted to the ministry. Both deserve to be named. Not in the
same voice.

### Threatened and vulnerable species — Grande Tourbière de Blainville
Collaborative citizen science inventory documenting the presence, distribution and status of species
observed in the peatland. New species recorded by naturalists and citizens are synchronized live
below.

#### Latest observations verified in the field:

### Fathead
49 individuals in the inventory corrected for captures in the Stablex file. Small fish of ponds and
slow-moving streams. Its abundance says: calm, warm water, not a trout river.

### Shadow of slime
4 captures. It is the emblematic fish of oxygen-poor peat bogs: it comes up to swallow the air.
Finding it here is more telling than a fishing trophy. The corrected report specifies that no brook
trout were caught.

### Five-spine stickleback
1 capture in the same patch. Kind of small vegetated environments. Le Devoir, citing company
documents, also speaks of nine species of fish in the sector: the complete list of these nine names
was not in the public extracts read for this file.

### Copper Knight
Endangered, endemic. Critical habitat in sections of the Mille Îles River, not in the acidic pools
of the peat bog. The link is hydrological: what leaves the complex ends in its river.

### Rock digger, sand darter, grass minnow
Fish with status of the river refuge. They talk about the seagrass beds and the sandy bottoms of the
corridor, not the carpet of sphagnum moss.

### Sphagnum moss
The City and naturalist descriptions agree: the ancient lake was invaded by sphagnum moss. It’s not
a species, it’s the profession of the place. Acidifies, fills with water, archives pollen, stores
carbon.

### Virginia Woodwardie
Rare fern from the peat bogs of southern Quebec, likely to be designated. Ecological studies confirm
a highly suitable habitat on the site, and field naturalists have reported observations to be
documented during botanical monitoring.

### Follicle sedge and Virginia St. John's wort
Targeted by the ministry in questions to Stablex, with precise inventory windows: sedge from
mid-July to early October, St. John's wort from August to September, woodwardie from June to
October. The fact that we require these passages speaks to the quality of the habitat, not yet the
result.

### Pitcher plant
Carnivorous plant emblematic of open peat bogs. The urns fill with rainwater and digest insects: an
elegant response to soil without nitrogen. Much photographed by the defenders of the site. Not yet
seen in a public ministerial table consulted here. Ecologically, its presence would be normal.

### Rossolis
Two sundews, glue traps on round or spatulate leaves. Same logic as the pitcher plant: the nitrogen
comes from midges, not from the soil. A single insect feeds the plant for weeks. Repeated citizen
reports.

### Labrador tea
Aromatic bog shrub. Leaves infused in much of the indigenous Northeast. If a single plant were to
link the botanical record and the indigenous record, it would be this one — provided we do not
invent an uncollected local ceremony for it.

### Cottongrass, kalmia, cassander, larch
The classic costume of a southern Quebec peat bog: cotton grass, kalmias, caliculated cassander,
andromeda, cranberry, tamarack and sometimes black spruce on the mounds, red maple in the swamps. To
be confirmed species by species. Do not check them off like an inventory.

### American carmantine
Threatened riverbank plant, most of whose Canadian population is linked to the Mille Îles River.
Immediate neighborhood, not the boggy heart.

### Four-toed salamander
Small, white belly with black stars, four toes on the hind legs. It lays eggs in sphagnum moss.
Radio-Canada cited it in 2016 among the species with status in the Fauvel lakes corridor. A youth
media shows her in 2026 as present in the Grande Tourbière (Great Peat Bog). Precarious status in
Quebec. It is probably the signature animal of the place, pending a published inventory.

### painted turtle
Le Devoir writes that the land intended to be razed is a habitat for the painted turtle, listed
under Canada's Species at Risk Act. Supporting photo in the article of March 19, 2025. Presence
among the most solid in the herpetological file.

### Snapping turtle
Large muddy bottom turtle, of concern in Canada. Citizen list of the sector, and species monitored
by the river refuge. Credible. Not a capture cited in the ministerial extracts read.

### Map turtle
River turtle, map on the shell, protected habitat in the refuge. Citizen lists also put it near the
peat bog. Its true home is the current and the islands, where it winters at the bottom, protected
from frost.

### wood turtle
Threatened. Le Devoir cites it among the theoretically protected animals which risk being killed by
the works, with a “like” which may only be an example of the category. It is not counted here as
confirmed on the lot, as long as an inventory mention is not public.

### Milk and green snakes
Two status snakes, cited by the citizen appeals of April 2025. The spotted snake is also a target of
the riverside refuge. The brown snake is named in the refuge regulations, not in the peat bog lists.

### The sky of the complex
Nearly 200 species of birds recorded in the project area, according to Le Devoir. On the site
itself, a ministerial report mentions 132 species, including 66% nesting and 34% probable or
possible nesting. Families mentioned: warblers, ducks, woodpeckers, birds of prey, shorebirds,
buntings, thrushes, herons. The Fauvel lakes alone are reported to have more than 100 species.

### Common Nightjar
Threat. Named by Le Devoir among the endangered species recorded in the area. Niche on the ground,
in openings. Spring deforestation falls exactly on schedule.

### Canada Warbler and Eastern Pewee
Two undergrowth birds in decline, cited in the same article. The Canada warbler is also followed by
the river refuge. Their presence says that the complex is not just a marsh: there is mature humid
forest, or fairly continuous for these species.

### Common Yellowthroat, Blue-headed Vireo
The common yellowthroat is considered a potential nester in the area, in decline due to loss of
habitat. The blue-headed vireo was photographed on the coveted ground. Neither are ornithologist’s
curiosities: they are birds of forest continuity.

### Bobolink
Threatened, bird of wet meadows. Noted at Fauvel lakes by amateur ornithologists, and shown in 2026
by youth media as a Grande Tourbière species. The wastelands and meadows of the complex suit it
better than the rounded bog.

### Least bittern
The smallest heron, threatened, dependent on cattail marshes. Target of the riverside refuge and
citizen lists in the area. Discreet to the point that you miss it if you don't listen at dawn.

### Great blue heron, chimney swift
The great blue heron is commonplace and magnificent on the river; citizens also put it near ponds.
The endangered chimney swift was cited in 2016 for the Fauvel corridor. It nests in chimneys and
large hollow trees, not in sphagnum moss.

### What we see at Fauvel lakes
Local notes, linked to eBird, mention kingfisher, kingfisher, tawny thrush, cuckoo, bobolink,
melodious vireo, buntings, warblers, ducks, waders. This is a sample of a public park, not the
secret of the inaccessible heart of the bog.

### Moose
Le Devoir confirms the moose in the ecosystem of approximately 6.2 km². A moose in a suburban bog,
half an hour from downtown, is already a political fact. He needs continuity. A landfill cell in the
middle of the complex is a fracture, not the loss of “a few trees.”

### Deer, beaver, muskrat, fox
Cited by petitions and naturalists. All ecologically expected. The beaver is also an engineer: its
dams can maintain exactly the water level on which the peat bog depends. Killing it or drying up its
pond is rewriting hydrology.

### Bats
Little brown bat, northern myotis, eastern pipistrelle: species at risk monitored in the wildlife
corridor of the Mille Îles River. Swamps and mature woodlands constitute prime hunting grounds for
these nocturnal insectivores.

### Monarch and rusty-spotted bumblebee
Two status insects tracked on the Mille Îles River. The monarch needs milkweed, rather in an open
environment than in an acidic bog. The rusty-patched bumblebee is on the brink of continental
extinction. Mentioning them reminds us that the corridor does not stop at vertebrates.

### What the number “742 species” cannot carry
A citizen mobilization in April 2025 claims that 742 species have been listed in the cell 6 sector.
The figure is circulating. There is no official total in the ministerial documents cited by the
press. The solid public totals are other: nearly 200 birds in the sector, 132 on the site in a
report, nine fish mentioned in the sector, three species named in the catch correction, 97 hectares
and more than 100 birds at Fauvel lakes, 92 species with status in the riverine refuge. A broad
citizen inventory can be true without being quotable as a state figure. To reject it out of
contempt, or to engrave it as truth, are two lazinesses.

### Why is this living being “from the south” and “from the north” at the same time?
The peat bogs of the alluvial plain, like those of Lanoraie further east on the north shore, are
refuges: they keep northern plants (dwarf birch, low willows) and southern plants (Virginia
woodwardie) far from the heart of their area. Blainville is in the same natural province. This is
why a suburban peat bog can contain flora that you would think were boreal, next to a highway. The
comparison with the Tourbières-de-Lanoraie ecological reserve is the best mirror available: same
family of environments, real protection there, industrial file here.

## The country does not stop at the city sign.

#### Sainte-Anne-des-Plaines and Mirabel
The track and wetlands continue towards the north-east, in a long agricultural plain. Mirabel has
lost immense wetlands to the benefit of the airport and crops. The Blainville complex is one of the
last large connected pieces. Jérôme Dupras, ecological economist, reminded us: in a region which has
already lost the majority of its natural environments, this sector is one of the rare areas of
connectivity that remain.

#### Boisbriand, Rosemère, Lorraine, Sainte-Thérèse
Same lordship, same clay, same parish detachments from the 20th century. Rosemère touches the refuge
of the islands. The Rosemère woodland and the footbridges to Darling Island are part of the same
waterfront network. To think “Blainville only” is to cut living things in two.

#### Laval, Jesus Island
Algonquin stops attested in 1674. Bank archaeology. Today, Rivière-des-Mille-Îles Park is the most
successful public gateway to understanding the corridor. Exploration center, kayaking, refuge
islands. It is there, more than in a dump parking lot, that we see the redhorse, the map turtle, the
great blue heron.

#### Saint-Eustache and Deux-Montagnes
1837. The river as a front. The church burned, the village pillaged. A few kilometers away,
Kanehsatà:ke, already a century-old mission, already a contested title. School history separates
“our” Patriots and “the” Natives. The terrain puts them on the same river, the same week.

#### Oka, Kanehsatà:ke, national park
Mission of 1721. Pine forest. Graveyard. Golf. 1990. Today, the Kanehsatà:ke powwow, one of the
oldest in Quebec, and still young indigenous tourism in the Lower Laurentians. The Oka National Park
occupies part of the landscape that the Sulpicians opened to resorts. To visit the park without
knowing who owns the pine forest next door is to miss half the place.

#### Kahnawà:ke and Akwesasne
Not adjoining the bog, but parts of the same nation. Kahnawà:ke was born from a Jesuit seigneury of
Sault-Saint-Louis, granted in 1680, since reduced. Current demands refuse to be confined within the
seigniorial perimeter. When a Blainville petition says “this territory is unceded”, it takes up this
position. It does not, in itself, create legal title to the municipal lot.

#### Terrebonne, Isle of Moulins
Where the Mille Îles River joins the Prairies. Bird sanctuary, seagrass beds, seasonal fishing ban
to protect spawning, permanent ban on the copper redhorse. The blue route, a navigable route,
reminds us that the Outaouais, the lake and the Mille Îles are a single route.

#### Lanoraie, the protected mirror
Tourbières-de-Lanoraie ecological reserve, in the alluvial plain, further east. Virginia woodwardie,
dwarf birch, moose, beaver, some 140 species of birds, and a bog butterfly, the Lanoraie elfin.
Proof that the State knows how to protect this type of environment when it decides to do so. Proof,
too, of what a complete inventory can reveal once it is financed.

## 2023–2026: the peat bog enters history through injury.

Stablex submits the rearrangement of cell 6. The BAPE holds a hearing. Report 371, submitted on
September 8, 2023, notes that the land is part of a corridor connecting two wetland complexes deemed
exceptional, and that the project would fragment it. The commission does not recommend
authorization. In September, the City adopted an ecological transition plan which calls for the
protection of peatlands.

Expert opinion from the ministry, June 2024, published only on April 4, 2025: the adjacent wetlands
have a high ecological value, a large part of which is included in the Lowlands Atlas. The ministry
writes in black and white the risk to the sustainability of the complex.

Despite the BAPE, Quebec tabled a special law, referred to in the press as the Loi 93 (Bill 93), and
forced the City to cede the land. Minister Maïté Blanchette-Vézina states on several occasions that
this is an industrial site without a peat bog “for a long time”. The Englobe study, obtained by Le
Devoir, describes 278,046 m² of wetlands on the ground, including 97,237 m² of peatlands,
approximately double the wetland area destroyed in the Northvolt file, a comparison made by the
newspaper. The table of ecological functions from the same study gives a high yield to the site's
peatlands for biodiversity, climate, water regulation and natural screen. However, Stablex speaks of
“low ecological value”.

Blainville and the CMM contest. An alternative proposal would have added 123 hectares of protected
wetlands on municipal land, to consolidate the Grande Tourbière. The Court rejects the request for
an injunction. The call follows. The destruction work begins. The decree still requires an update of
the wildlife inventory: at the time of authorization, the State did not have all the data. Fisheries
and Oceans Canada indicated in March that federal authorization for fish habitat had not been
requested. The ministry then requires at least fifteen years of monitoring of the impacts on
adjacent environments.

The “Save the Grande Tourbière” petition has around 47,000 signatures at the beginning of 2026.
Dozens of municipalities and the UMQ support Blainville. Scientists sign letters. On April 8, 2025,
Le Devoir headlines on the peat bog several thousand years old. In 2026, William Guindon, then a
teenager, filed a complaint with the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human
Rights, on behalf of future generations, after having exhausted, he says, the remedies here. La
Presse meets him on July 3, 2026 in the Fauvel lakes woodland. The Aces of Info present him at 14
years old in July 2026. The official site then says he is 15 years old.

Le Devoir reports that water and sediment samples, taken by Climat Québec and the Coalition of
Blainville Citizens near the plant, contain several toxic metals. Citizen campaign relayed by a
major daily newspaper, not a conclusion from the ministry. It is enough to say that the chemical
question is not theoretical, and that it goes beyond deforestation alone.

The government's position, to be fair, can be summed up in three sentences. Quebec would need a
capacity to bury stabilized hazardous materials, with cell 5 approaching its end. The land belonging
to the City would, according to the minister, already be industrial. The special law would avoid a
“break in service”. The position of the studies, including those of the ministry and the company's
agent, is based on three others. There is peat bog on and around the lot. It is exceptional on the
scale of the Lowlands. Destroying it or drying it out is not reversible on the scale of a human
life, or even several.

## Knowledge and data to deepen together.

Radiocarbon dating of the base of the peat bog, exhaustive pollen diagrams, precise assessment of
the carbon stock and the complete floristic inventory are among the essential independent scientific
projects to be pursued to protect this environment.

The ancestral toponymy and living history of these lands are part of the oral tradition of the
families of Kanehsatà:ke, Kahnawà:ke and surrounding nations. This traditional knowledge is not
measured by the yardstick of colonial administrative archives: it is listened to from communities,
with full respect for their sovereignty.

### A program, if anyone wants to continue
  ▸ Gesture
  ▸ For what
  ▸ With whom
  · Peat core, dating, pollen, macroremains
  · Transform “around 6000 years” into a real history of climate, fires, land clearings
  · University laboratory, and agreement on sampling: a core is a wound, even a small one
  · Request for the Inventory of Archaeological Sites in Quebec
  · Know what is already recorded on the banks of the Rivière aux Chiens and Mille Îles, without inventing villages
  · Archaeologists, and nations concerned before any excavation
  · Oral history
  · The silence of archives is not the silence of families
  · Kanehsatà:ke, Kahnawà:ke, Algonquin communities of Lac des Deux Montagnes. On their terms, not extraction
  · Publish inventories
  · Remove cash from “reported” or “industrial secret” status
  · Ministry, City, naturalists, eBird, iNaturalist
  · Independent hydrological monitoring
  · The ministry requires 15 years of follow-up from the company. Monitoring that does not depend on the promoter changes trust
  · University, CMM, watershed groups

Boisé des lacs Fauvel, rue Legault and rue Omer-DeSerres: the fairest public threshold. Boisé du
Plan Bouchard: war and swamp in the same path. Parc de la Rivière-des-Mille-Îles, in Laval: the
corridor in readable version. Kanehsatà:ke: only by what the community offers, powwow, market,
indigenous tourism, and not by going to “see the pine forest” as a setting. Do not enter the
industrial site. Do not leave the footbridges during the nesting period. Do not pick. The pitcher
plant is not a memory.

## Where does each floor come from?

Research stopped on September 25, 2026. Open sources, in French and English. The PDFs of the
environmental register and the BAPE vault are cited by the public extracts and by the articles which
reproduce them. They have not all been reread page by page.
- Ville de Blainville, pages History, Environment, Blainville tells. Concession of 1683, city status
-   on June 29, 1968, 19.2 km² of wooded areas, 9.86 km² of wetlands, peat bogs “which could date back
-   almost 6,000 years”.
- Statistics Canada, 2021 census, Blainville subdivision: 59,819 inhabitants, 54.97 km² of land area
-   in the file consulted.
- Le Devoir, Alexandre Shields, March to April 2025, notably the articles of March 19, March 28, April
-   2, April 3 and April 8, 2025, on wetlands, fish, birds, the ministerial report and the
-   6,000-year-old peat bog. Article from June 12, 2026 on metals in citizen samples.
- Ministry of the Environment, environmental analysis report and expert opinion of June 2024, file
-   3211-21-014, cited by Le Devoir. 622.7 ha complex, high ecological value, Lowlands Atlas, carbon
-   sink, 15-year monitoring.
- Englobe, 2023-2024 characterizations filed for Stablex, cited by Le Devoir and the register: 278,046
-   m² of wetlands including 97,237 m² of peat bogs; high ecological functions; potential habitats for
-   woodwardie, follicle sedge, Virginia St. John's wort.
- Correction to the file, environmental register, document 3211-21-014-13: catches of fathead minnow
-   (49), mud umbre (4), five-spined stickleback (1); no brook trout.
- BAPE, report 371, investigation into cell 6, submitted in September 2023. Recommendation not to
-   authorize; fragmentation of a corridor between two exceptional complexes. Vault of BAPE.
- La Presse, Valérie Simard, July 3, 2026, “Protection of a peat bog in Blainville. A teenager's fight
-   at the UN.
- Les As de l’info, July 17, 2026, interview with William Guindon; four-toed salamander and bobolink.
- Radio-Canada / RCI, 2016, Fauvel lakes, four-toed salamander, chimney swift. Echo of the North
-   Shore, 2014 cleanup. Hydro-Québec Foundation and Nature-Action Québec, 2017 inauguration.
- Stories from home / Virtual museum, Prairie and Thousand Islands rivers: Dalmas 1674, Algonquin
-   cabin, artifacts.
- Wikiworld and toponymic notices, Mille Îles River: Makigôtekw, artifacts estimated from 3000 BCE to
-   1000, to be treated as secondary synthesis.
- Wikipedia and municipal notices of Oka; pages on Kanesatake and the 1990 crisis; Kahnawake, claim to
-   the seigneury of Sault-Saint-Louis and unceded traditional territory, Radio-Canada Indigenous
-   Spaces, 2021.
- McGill, Indigenous Initiatives, recognition of Tiohtià:ke. UQTR, educational document Laurentides
-   2025, Weskarini and Kanien’kehá:ka ancestral territory to the south.
- Tourism Basses-Laurentides, pages on Kanehsatà:ke. Politics of Tioweró:ton, Kahnawà:ke.
- 1837.qc.ca and chronologies of the Patriots: march of November 30, 1837 towards Oka, expedition
-   towards Kahnawà:ke, battle of Saint-Eustache on December 14.
- Rivière-des-Mille-Îles Wildlife Refuge, Wikipedia and Éco-Nature; DFO advice on critical habitat for
-   the copper redhorse.
- Tourbières-de-Lanoraie ecological reserve, file MELCCFP (Québec Ministry of the Environment, the
-   Fight against Climate Change, Wildlife and Parks), as a protected analogue.
- Payette and Rochefort (dir.), Ecology of Quebec-Labrador peatlands, Presses de l'Université Laval,
-   2001. Occhietti and Richard, 2003, chronology of the Champlain Sea. Archéo-Québec, prehistory of
-   Quebec. Marie-Victorin, Flore laurentienne, 1935, for the name of the Hochelaga archipelago.
- Change.org and citizen pages of the Grande Tourbière: lists of species, to be read as reports, not
-   as a certified inventory. The figure of 742 species remains unverified.
- Waymarking and local heritage notices on the Bouchard Plan: useful for the scale of the military
-   complex, to be cross-checked with federal archives before specific historical use.
- Édith Mora Castelan, direct details on traditional Kanien'kehá:ka governance, the fundamental
-   distinction between the Longhouse (Clan Mothers, Kanonsonnkweonkwe / Kannosonhkwe'ón:we) and the
-   band system imposed by the Indian Act, and explanatory webinar (video recording ↗).

This file is a citizen documentary research carried out in a spirit of sincere solidarity and deep
respect for indigenous peoples. It never replaces the voice of nations, traditional councils of the
Long House or elders. Any correction, toponymic precision or transmission from communities and
knowledge keepers absolutely takes precedence over this text and is welcomed with humility and
gratitude.

# News & Logbook
Official articles, legal analyzes and citizen reflections by William Guindon published directly
online.

## Bilateral meeting with Nadine Le Gal (MP for Blainville): Eco-royalty of $1,000/tonne, Loi 93 and citizen action calendar

On November 6, 2026, William Guindon held an official bilateral meeting with Nadine Le Gal, Member
of Parliament for Blainville, and Sylvain Pagé, alongside scientist Daniel Green and Édith Mora
Castelan (first nations citizen expert, field watchdog in residual materials management and
environmental rights). Absolute priority given to Grande Tourbière (Great Peat Bog), eco-royalty
project of $1,000/t, denunciation of federal inertia, creation of a council in January 2027 and
inclusion of First Nations.

## Documentary investigation: What the Official Registry of Lobbyists of Canada reveals during full examination of the file SEM-26-003

While Environment and Climate Change Canada left 10 citizen questions on cadmium unanswered for 119
days, an official meeting was held between the associate assistant deputy minister and the Stablex
representative on the subject of cell 6.

## Parliamentary meetings in November: Nadine Le Gal (MP for Blainville) and Madeleine Chenette (federal MP) listening to the citizen file

Two high-level meetings are confirmed for November 2026 with the region's parliamentary
representatives: Nadine Le Gal, new provincial MP for Blainville (who was keen to plan this exchange
even before the official opening of her constituency office), and Madeleine Chenette, federal MP for
Thérèse-De Blainville in the House of Commons of Canada.

## Canada's official response (SEM-26-003): The federal government is attempting a procedural rejection in the face of evidence of contamination of Grande Tourbière

In-depth analysis of the formal response (72-RSP) filed on October 16, 2026 by Canada before the CCE
(Commission for Environmental Cooperation): request for rejection or postponement invoking the
petition to the BVG, total absence of dispute over the releases of cadmium (320x) and the
destruction of the peat bog, and opening of the CCE examination under article 24.28 of the ACEUM
(Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement).

## Field reporting: Wildlife incidents and illegal dumping in the Plan-Bouchard sector

As part of field monitoring and ongoing documentation of local natural environments in Blainville, a
formal report was made concerning recent wildlife incidents as well as the presence of wild deposits
observed in the Plan-Bouchard woodland sector.

## Environmental Petition No. 546: Official registration with the Auditor General of Canada (CEDD)

Official registration of environmental petition no. 15-546 in the catalog of the Office of the
Auditor General of Canada (Commissioner of the Environment and Sustainable Development - CEDD),
questioning ECCC, DFO and Health Canada on the application of federal laws in Blainville.

## SEM-26-003 explained simply: The complete and accessible file on Grande Tourbière de Blainville

Publication of a large, popularized documentary summary in 12 parts simply explaining the history,
the allegations, the CCE decision of August 17, 2026, the actors and the numerical traps.

## Large documentary file: The peat bog that remembers — Indigenous history, ecology & water territories

Publication of a major independent documentary synthesis exploring 6,000 years of memory of the
Grande Tourbière de Blainville (Great Blainville Peat Bog), the history of indigenous peoples
(Kanien'kehá:ka, Anishinaabeg, Haudenosaunee) and the water territories of the Mille Îles River
(Makigôtekw).

## Official confirmation of the CCE: First minor author in the history of the SEM mechanism since 1994

The Secretariat of the Commission de coopération environnementale (CCE / ACEUM) formally certifies
in writing that William Guindon is the very first minor in 32 years of history (since 1994) to file
a citizen communication.

## Public clarification notice: Lack of collaboration and strict independence

Official clarification from William Guindon recalling the complete independence and the strictly
individual and self-financed nature of the file SEM-26-003 submitted to CCE, dispelling any
allegation of partisan aid or external co-editing.

## Advocating for the legal autonomy of adolescents: breaking the glass ceiling of institutional ageism

By defending complex environmental issues, William Guindon highlights the blind spot of bureaucratic
paternalism and institutional ageism that deprives adolescents aged 14 to 17 of their full civic and
legal capacity.

## Official launch of the search engine for supporting documents and the ZIP archive (96%+ of the file)

Uploading of the internal full-text search engine (OCR) of all the supporting documents of the
SEM-26-003 file, accompanied by a downloadable ZIP archive of more than 96% of the supporting
documents.

## Citizen Survey: Position of political parties on Stablex (Cell 6) and the decision CCE SEM-26-003

Methodology of the citizen survey and collection of positions of provincial political groups (CAQ,
PLQ, PQ, QS, PCQ, Climat Québec) on the Stablex file and the CCE ordinance.

## Behind the scenes of the filming with Lab22 at Lac Fauvel: raising the citizen voice

Return to a day of filming at Lac Fauvel with the Lab22 team, the opportunity to approach from a
more human and personal angle the motivations behind my civic submission to the CCE.

## Historic victory: CCE Secretariat orders official response from Canada

Return to the positive determination of August 17, 2026 made by Commission de coopération
environnementale (Commission for Environmental Cooperation) in file SEM-26-003.

## Submission of the revised 15-page submission to the Commission

Finalization and official submission of the consolidated version of the citizen submission
SEM-26-003 integrating scientific and legal data.

# Press releases
Official publications and declarations sent to editorial offices and press agencies.

## SEM-26-003: Official filing of William Guindon's observations refuting Canada's request for rejection or postponement

BLAINVILLE (Québec), October 17, 2026 – Less than 24 hours after the Government of Canada filed its
official response (exhibit 72-RSP) requesting to terminate or suspend the examination of citizen
communication SEM-26-003, William Guindon formally transmitted to the Secretariat of the Commission
de coopération environnementale (CCE / ACEUM (Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement)), to the
attention of its director of legal affairs Mr. Paolo Solano, his memorandum of Observations refuting
point by point Ottawa's procedural maneuver.

#### THE 7 LEGAL AND FACTUAL MEANS FILED AT CCE
In a memorandum rigorously structured around the ACEUM treaties and the constant jurisprudence of
the Secretariat, the author demonstrates why Canada's request for closure or suspension must be
entirely rejected:
- • 1. Invocability absent from Article 24.27(4)(a): Canada cannot invoke this article because
-   environmental petition No. 0546 filed with the Auditor General (CEDD/BVG) is neither a judicial or
-   administrative procedure initiated by the Party, nor a quasi-judicial procedure. The BVG has no
-   power to sanction or verify the accuracy of ministerial responses.
- • 2. Guideline 7.5 discretionary and protective: This provision is intended to protect the private
-   remedies of the author of external interference, and not to provide a procedural loophole for the
-   respondent Party. The two approaches are complementary and non-conflicting.
- • 3. Previous decision of the Secretariat (paras. 66-79 of August 17, 2026): The Secretariat has
-   already expressly ruled on the coexistence of approaches and the criterion of reasonable time
-   (decision A24.27(2)(3)/SEM/26-003/62/DET, citing Tijuana, Nazas and Pollution by ships). Going
-   back on it would constitute an unjustified reversal.
- • 4. Fundamental difference in nature and scope: The CCE (Commission for Environmental Cooperation)
-   examination concerns the application of 17 provisions from 5 federal environmental laws (LCOM,
-   RCOM, SARA, Fisheries Act, CEPA). Petition No. 0546 consists of 24 questions of ministerial
-   information without any allegation of failure to implement.
- • 5. Disproportionate burden contrary to the practice of CCE: Requiring the prior exhaustion of
-   simple questions of information would create a disproportionate burden contrary to the objectives
-   of the Agreement and to jurisprudence (Loggerhead turtle, Fairview Terminal, Agricultural
-   burning), particularly with regard to minor citizens acting without legal representation.
- • 6. Postponement to November 10 indefinite: The postponement requested by Canada is by nature
-   indeterminate, since section 22 of the Auditor General Act authorizes ministers to unilaterally
-   extend their response deadline by simple written notice.
- • 7. No enforcement measures for cell 6 on the merits: On the merits, Canada's response does not
-   mention any enforcement measures for federal environmental legislation relating to cell 6 of the
-   Stablex site (paras. 54, 64, 76).
> “Environmental petition no. 0546 and citizen communication SEM-26-003 are not rival or contradictory
> approaches: they are complementary. Canada is trying to transform a simple citizen mechanism for
> access to information into an international inadmissibility barrier. Section 24.27(4)(a) does not
> allow a government to use its own delays to silence an alert about cadmium and the destruction of a
> peat bog. » — William Guindon, author of communication SEM-26-003

#### CONCLUSION AND REQUEST TO THE SECRETARIAT (ART. 24.28 ACEUM)
The author formally requested the CCE Secretariat to:
- 1. Reject the request to terminate the examination of communication SEM-26-003;
- 2. Reject the request to postpone the examination;
- 3. Continue to review the communication pursuant to Article 24.28(1) of ACEUM with a view to
-   recommending the development of an independent factual record.

## Canada's response filed to CCE: Ottawa attempts procedural rejection in the face of evidence of contamination of Grande Tourbière de Blainville

BLAINVILLE (Quebec), October 16, 2026 – On the deadline prescribed by Commission de coopération
environnementale (CCE / ACEUM), the Government of Canada formally submitted its official response
(exhibit 72-RSP) to the Secretariat of the international organization in Montreal, which
acknowledged receipt the same day under the signature of its director of legal affairs, Paolo Solano
(exhibit 73-REC ). The 15-year-old Quebec teenager William Guindon, author of the citizen
communication SEM-26-003, denounces a procedural avoidance maneuver on the part of Ottawa which
chooses to invoke its own internal administrative deadlines to try to escape examination of its
environmental inaction.

#### A PROCEDURAL DENITION OF EVIDENCE OF POLLUTION
In a succinct two-page brief, the federal government does not devote a single line to contesting the
scientific and environmental findings supported in the citizen file. Its strategy is entirely based
on a procedural inadmissibility:
- • Formal request for rejection or suspension: Canada formally requests the Secretariat of CCE to
-   terminate the examination of the communication SEM-26-003 or to suspend its processing.
- • The duplication argument (Petition 0546 to the OAG): Canada claims that the international review
-   is “premature” due to the filing, on June 29, 2026, by William Guindon of environmental petition
-   No. 0546 with the Commissioner of the Environment and Sustainable Development (CESD) of the Office
-   of the Auditor General of Canada.
- • The mandatory ministerial deadline of November 10, 2026: Sent on July 13, 2026 to the ministers of
-   Environment and Climate Change Canada (ECCC) and Fisheries and Oceans Canada (DFO), this petition
-   statutorily obliges federal ministers to table their responses to Parliament within a strict
-   deadline of 120 days (s. 22 of the Auditor General Act), i.e. no later than November 10, 2026 .
- • Guideline 7.5 of the CCE: Canada argues that investigating the international complaint before this
-   date would constitute unnecessary overlap with ongoing domestic remedies.

#### WHAT CANADA DOES NOT DISPUTE: UNREFUTED SCIENTIFIC FACTS
Legal examination of Exhibit 72-RSP reveals what Ottawa has chosen to keep quiet:
- • Cadmium up to 320 times the standards: The critical exceedances documented in connecting waterways
-   and the documented discharge of 1,300 liters of effluent (certified by Bureau Veritas) are not the
-   subject of any government denial.
- • Destruction of 278,000 m² of wetlands: The encroachment for Cell 6 of Stablex, condemned by report
-   BAPE 371 and imposed under gag order by Loi 93 (L.Q. 2025, c. 7), remains without federal
-   ecological justification.
- • Zero proof of authorization or inspection: The federal government has not produced any proof of
-   ministerial authorization issued under section 36(3) of the Fisheries Act or the Species at Risk
-   Act (SARA).
> “When a government is caught failing to apply its own laws, its first reaction is not to correct
> pollution: it is to argue that the clock is going too fast. Pretending that CCE must stop because
> another federal ministry has not finished writing its letter by November 10 is using bureaucracy as
> a shield against the ecological emergency. » — William Guindon, author of the communication
> SEM-26-003
> “Before an international court, silence constitutes confession. Canada has not denied a single
> milligram of cadmium, has not contested a single square meter of peat bog destroyed, and has been
> unable to show any proper authorization for Cell 6. If the government had respected its laws, it
> would have proven it in two pages. He preferred to ask to close the file. » — William Guindon

#### NEXT STEPS: ARTICLE 24.28 AND NOVEMBER 10 CALENDAR
In accordance with article 24.28 of the ACEUM, the file now enters its substantive examination
phase:

The CCE Secretariat must determine whether Canada's response provides satisfactory clarification or
whether the alleged violations justify recommending to the Ministers of the Environment of Canada,
the United States and Mexico the development of an independent Factual Record. At the same time,
William Guindon is awaiting the mandatory written response from federal ministers to Petition 0546
by November 10, 2026.

William Guindon is available for interviews and media interventions on the developments of October
16, 2026.

## 15-year-old calls on Canada to enforce its own environmental laws

BLAINVILLE (Quebec), October 9, 2026 – In seven days, Canada will have to officially respond to
communication SEM-26-003 filed by William Guindon, a 15-year-old Canadian teenager living in
Blainville. The Secretariat of the Commission de coopération environnementale (CCE) deemed this
communication admissible within the framework of the Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement (ACEUM)
and Canada's response is expected no later than October 16, 2026. The young person accuses the
federal government of not enforcing its own environmental laws in the Stablex file, in Blainville.

The communication concerns the activities of cell no. 6 of Stablex Canada Inc., a hazardous
materials burial project in the Grande Tourbière de Blainville (Great Blainville Peat Bog) sector,
on the site of the former Camp Bouchard. The land of cell no. 6 (lot 6 695 053 of the Quebec
cadastre) was expropriated from Ville de Blainville (City of Blainville) by Loi 93 (L.Q. 2025, c.
7). William Guindon denounces the threats posed by this project on endangered species and on the
wetlands of the Grande Tourbière de Blainville and Lockhead Creek, in the Mille-Îles River sector,
as well as the inaction of the authorities. He alleges failure to apply the Fisheries Act, the
Species at Risk Act, federal rules on migratory birds and the Canadian Environmental Protection Act.

-   October 16, that is to say in seven days.
- • September 25, 2026: the Secretariat of the CCE confirms that “since 1994, this is the first time
-   that an author has indicated to us that they are a minor when submitting a file to the CCE”.
- • Federal inspection report no. 402462402 (April 2025): an unmarked 10 m protection zone along a
-   watercourse, heavy machinery observed 5 m from this watercourse and invasive exotic species not
-   controlled on the site.
> “When a 15-year-old asks questions of public interest, procedural barriers are put in place. But

> “The federal army left a heavy environmental legacy on this site for decades. Today, Ottawa

> regarding the site of the former Camp Bouchard

PAGE 12/50 · https://williamguindon.me/contamination.html
Title : Cadmium contamination in Blainville: Sampling Dec. 2023, WaterShed & Lacs Fauvel
==============================================================================

## 1. Sampling results from December 2023 (Cadmium at 320×)

Cadmium (Cd) is one of the most toxic heavy metals for living organisms. Proven carcinogen,
bioaccumulative and persistent in perpetuity in the environment, it is subject to the most rigorous
environmental standards in Canada and Quebec. The criterion for the protection of aquatic life
(chronic effect in fresh water) established by the Ministry of the Environment of Quebec (MELCCFP)
is set at 0.00025 mg/L (i.e. 0.25 µg/L).

During the independent sampling campaign conducted in December 2023, samples were collected using a
rigorous upstream-downstream observation grid. While the stations located upstream of the forest
presented concentrations below the analytical detection limit (< 0.0001 mg/L), the stations located
in the ditches receiving runoff and resurgence water downstream from cells 1 to 4 displayed alarming
values:
  ▸ Sampling station
  ▸ Location
  ▸ Cadmium measured (mg/L)
  ▸ Criterion MELCCFP (aquatic life)
  ▸ Relative deviation
  · ST-01 (Upstream witness)
  · Northwest bog
  · < 0.0001 mg/L
  · 0.00025 mg/L
  · Compliant (natural background)
  · ST-04 (Periphery)
  · West ditch cell 3
  · 0.012 mg/L
  · 48× the criterion
  · ST-07 (Direct downstream)
  · South discharge ditch
  · 0.080 mg/L
  · 320× the criterion
  · ST-09 (Flow)
  · Stream towards Fauvel
  · 0.018 mg/L
  · 72× the criterion

## 2. The WaterShed Monitoring hydrogeological study (June 2026)

One of the traditional arguments put forward to justify the burial of hazardous waste in this sector
is based on the presumed presence of an impermeable marine clay base from the Champlain Sea.
However, the in-depth field study carried out by WaterShed Monitoring in June 2026 invalidated this
geological simplification:
- Heterogeneity of loose deposits: The thickness of the clay layer is highly variable and in places
-   presents discontinuities (hydrogeological windows) occupied by conductive silty sands.
- Hydraulic gradient towards the southwest: The underground hydraulic load naturally pushes pore water
-   from the peaty plateau towards the surrounding depressions, with an actual percolation speed
-   higher than the developer's theoretical modeling.
- Physico-chemical interaction with peat bog water: Peat bog water, naturally acidic and rich in humic
-   and fulvic acids, accelerates the dissolution of heavy metals trapped in alkaline residues when
-   they come into contact with environmental seeps.

## 3. The ecological vulnerability of Lacs Fauvel

Former sand and gravel pits restored by the community, the Fauvel lakes have been transformed over
the decades into an exceptional ecological refuge in the heart of Blainville. This area is home to a
rich community of fish (bass, yellow perch, pike), turtles and more than 150 recorded species of
birds.

Hydrological data demonstrate that the peripheral drainage network of the Grande Tourbière (Great
Peat Bog) flows by gravity towards the tributaries which feed the Fauvel lakes basin before joining
the Mille Îles River, the source of drinking water for more than 400,000 residents of the northern
suburbs of Montreal. Any uncontrolled toxic release at Stablex level indirectly contaminates this
irreplaceable collective heritage.

## 4. The official position of Stablex Canada Inc. and second opinion

For the sake of objectivity and journalistic rigor, it is appropriate to set out in detail the
arguments formulated by Stablex and its consultant experts in their documents filed with the
Ministry of the Environment (MELCCFP):
- The concept of stabilized waste: Stablex maintains that heavy metals are integrated into the
-   crystalline network of calcium hydrosilicates during the cement reaction, making them insoluble
-   even in the presence of infiltration water.
- Official piezometer monitoring: The company maintains a network of around thirty regulatory
-   piezometers inspected periodically and maintains that the data transmitted to the ministry
-   respects the thresholds prescribed by its operating authorizations.
- Hypothesis of historical history: The northern sector of Blainville hosted the Bouchard Plan weapons
-   installations during the Second World War. Stablex raises the possibility that old embankments
-   unrelated to its activities explain certain specific anomalies in the surface ditches.

The independent experts, however, retort that the chemical signature of the samples (cadmium/zinc
ratio and presence of concentrated sulfates characteristic of the Stablex neutralization process)
does not correspond in any way to the military explosives of the Bouchard Plan, but rather to the
hydrometallurgy residues treated in the factory.

## 5. Legal implications: Fisheries Act and remedies CCE

In Canadian federal law, section 36(3) of the Fisheries Act establishes an absolute and strict
prohibition: it is prohibited to deposit or authorize the deposit of a harmful substance in waters
where fish live, or in a place where it risks entering such waters.

By demonstrating the existence of hydrographic connections between the ditches of the peat bog and
the fish habitats of Lacs Fauvel and the Rivière des Mille Îles, the contamination data places the
Government of Canada before its international responsibilities of monitoring and sanctions, which
are the subject of the formal procedure underway before the Secretariat of the CCE (Commission for
Environmental Cooperation).

Consult the certificates of analysis in the 222 evidentiary documents ↗ Read the file Loi 93 (Bill
93) ↗

Title : Communications & Correspondence Policy — William Guindon

# Communications, Correspondence and Public Dissemination Policy
Official terms governing the retention, anonymization of private correspondence and dissemination in
the public interest of communications addressed to William Guindon.

### Preamble & Ethical Rigor
As part of the independent citizen approach and public procedures surrounding SEM-26-003
communication with the Commission de coopération environnementale (CCE / ACEUM (Canada–United
States–Mexico Agreement)), transparency, documentary rigor and preservation of privacy constitute
cardinal requirements. This policy establishes a strict balance between the right to anonymity of
private correspondence and the duty to inform the public regarding statements emanating from
institutional or professional representatives in the exercise of their functions.

## 1. Scope of the policy

This policy governs all correspondence sent to William Guindon and its legal representatives,
including, but not limited to, exchanges occurring by email, LinkedIn, Messenger or any other
electronic communication platform. By interacting through these channels, the interlocutor takes
note of the processing and transparency methods set out below.

## 2. Processing, archiving and right of capture

For the purposes of documentary memory, factual monitoring or preservation of rights in the event of
a legal dispute, William Guindon expressly reserves the right to preserve, archive and capture (in
particular by screen capture, textual backup, audio or audiovisual recording in accordance with
applicable laws) the full content of the exchanges received.

## 3. Private exchanges: commitment to full anonymization

In the event that the content of an exchange relating to private correspondence is the subject of a
summary, analysis or public dissemination:
- Strict depersonalization: William Guindon undertakes to carry out irreversible and complete
-   anonymization of the interlocutor.
- Removal of identifiers: Names, direct contact details, profile photos, employers, as well as any
-   contextual circumstantial details likely to allow the direct or indirect identification of the
-   person will be systematically obscured.

## 4. Communications with public figures and professionals in the exercise of their functions

When communicating with representatives acting in their office, institutional mandate or profession
(including, but not limited to: elected politicians, MPs, scientists, academics, legal experts or
representatives of non-governmental organizations):
- Broadcasting in the public interest: William Guindon reserves the right to make public, summarize or
-   faithfully quote the written remarks, formal declarations or audiovisual extracts transmitted, as
-   long as they are of legitimate public interest and directly concern the exercise of their official
-   functions or their civic mandate.
- Rigor and loyalty: Any publication of this nature is made with strict respect for the integrity of
-   the statements (without alteration of meaning or misleading editing), for the exclusive purposes
-   of information or public debate, and without intention of causing undue harm to the reputation or
-   security of the interlocutor.

## 5. Express notice of confidentiality

Any interlocutor wishing that their communications remain strictly confidential, “under the seal of
secrecy” or not intended to be summarized, is required to notify them formally and in writing as
soon as the exchange begins.

### Official Contact Channels & Details
For any formal transmission, ethical question or request relating to the application of this policy:

Official email: contact@williamguindon.me

Session encrypted channel: 05dc60b62a6ed477b1f0dc5ce1b6a9db7603bf39f1a0efe13c68d63a6cb8a7c072

Title : Information Ethics Charter — William Guindon

# Charter of Information Ethics & Rules for the Media
Principles of documentary rigor of the site and ethical rules with which the media and journalists
must comply

### Clarification on the citizen approach and the purpose of this charter
William Guindon is not a professional journalist. He is an engaged citizen, student and author of
the international citizen communication SEM-26-003 filed with the Commission de coopération
environnementale (CCE / ACEUM (Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement)). This charter pursues a dual
objective: 1. Define the framework of documentary integrity, factual truth and information ethics
that this public register imposes in the processing of documents and data in the file. 2. Propose to
journalists, editorial staff and media professionals a framework of loyal and respectful
collaboration, based on recognized ethical standards of the press (in particular the Ethics Guide of
the Quebec Press Council), particularly adapted to the expression of a minor citizen.

## 1. Citizen status, scope of application and evidentiary nature

This charter governs all of William Guindon's actions, citizen field surveys, open letters, advocacy
efforts, official correspondence and publications, as well as the williamguindon.me site which
constitutes its official public documentary register. These initiatives respond to a goal of
legitimate public interest (procedure CCE (Commission for Environmental Cooperation) SEM-26-003,
protection of Grande Tourbière de Blainville (Great Blainville Peat Bog)), carried out by a citizen
under 15 years of age with usual parental consent for his public interventions.

## 2. Requirement for rigor, accuracy and verification

- Facts verified before dissemination: Any factual assertion is based on authenticated primary
-   documents, documents filed (BAPE, CCE, MELCCFP (Québec Ministry of the Environment, the Fight
-   against Climate Change, Wildlife and Parks)) or substantiated scientific data.
- Strict distinction between facts and opinions: Citizen analyzes or positions are rigorously
-   distinguished from observed legal and scientific data.
- Data integrity: The figures, procedural dates (notably the federal deadline of October 16, 2026),
-   surface areas and chemical findings are not subject to any alteration or extrapolation.
- Transparent rectification: Any demonstrated inaccuracy is corrected without delay and explicitly.

## 3. Principles of collaboration proposed to journalists and media

In order to preserve the quality of public debate and the rigor of media treatment, the site invites
journalists and editorial staff to observe the usual ethical principles:
- Accuracy of quotations: Represent the words and declarations of William Guindon faithfully, in their
-   context of expression.
- Respect for minors: Conducting interviews in compliance with the ethical rules applicable to minors
-   and usual parental consent.
- Attribution of sources: Cite the primary documents from file SEM-26-003 or the public register when
-   they are used as a documentary basis.
- Compliance with embargoes: Honor embargo agreements agreed upon when previously transmitting
-   unpublished documents.

## 4. Independence and financial integrity

- The site and its author do not have any guidelines, influence or paid lobbying mandate, partisan
-   affiliation or interference from any political party, government or corporate interest group.
- The SEM-26-003 citizen register, research and site are self-funded without monetization, monetary
-   donations from the public or sponsorship. The only remuneration received by William Guindon comes
-   from legitimate professional fees for his conferences, workshops or public education
-   interventions, without any right of inspection being granted over his investigation files.

## 5. Fairness of approach and open action

- William Guindon acts openly and under his true identity, in his capacity as a committed citizen and
-   author of the international appeal SEM-26-003.
- The site prohibits harassment, personal attacks and intimidation against anyone. The debate focuses
-   exclusively on facts, environmental law and the public interest.

## 6. Strengthened protection of privacy and minors

- Respect for the privacy, dignity and reputation of individuals in accordance with article 35 of Code
-   civil du Québec (Civil Code of Québec) and article 5 of the Quebec Charter of Human Rights and
-   Freedoms.
- Strict prohibition on disseminating personal data of third parties obtained illegally or without an
-   overriding reason of public interest.
- Reinforced protection of the private sphere of William Guindon and his family (see confidentiality
-   policy).

## 7. Public interest and proportionality

- All publications are part of a framework of major public interest: population health, preservation
-   of biodiversity, integrity of peatlands and application of national and international
-   environmental laws.
- The tone remains documented, proportionate and free from defamation.

## 8. Technological transparency and supervision of AI

- Total transparency regarding the technical or algorithmic assistance tools used, under exclusive
-   human supervision and systematic verification (see AI ethics policy).

## 9. Protection of sources and warning of legal limits

The site scrupulously respects the confidentiality agreed with citizens or whistleblowers. However,
in accordance with Canadian and Quebec legal reality:
- The protection of sources is not absolute in law in the absence of professional journalist status
-   recognized by a court, the site never promises absolute legal immunity which it cannot guarantee.
- Whistleblowers are directed to secure channels and end-to-end encryption (see the OpSec guide for
-   whistleblowers and Nostr messaging).

## 10. Right of rectification and response

Any person, organization or institution cited on this site has the right to factual rectification
and the right to reply, exercisable in writing. Requests based on tangible evidence are processed
diligently and in good faith.

## 11. Scope and application

This charter constitutes williamguindon.me's commitment to integrity and the reference framework
required of all partner or observer information media.

This charter is an independent policy of williamguindon.me, published as a public commitment to
ethical rigor.

## Bilateral meeting with Nadine Le Gal, Member of Parliament for Blainville, and Sylvain Pagé

On November 6, 2026, William Guindon held an in-depth bilateral working session with the new MP for
the Blainville constituency in Assemblée nationale du Québec (National Assembly of Québec), Nadine
Le Gal, and Sylvain Pagé. Showing a keen sense of listening to citizens, the MP held this exchange
even before having opened her permanent constituency office.

### Delegation & Participants at the working table

### The 5 major advances and established directions
Nadine Le Gal formally reaffirmed that the safeguarding of the Grande Tourbière de Blainville (Great
Blainville Peat Bog) and the critical examination of Stablex landfill activities constitute the
number one priority of her constituency mandate. The MP demonstrated rigorous listening to the 222
evidentiary documents placed in the public register.

At the heart of the regulatory and economic proposals, the delegation presented the establishment of
a dissuasive eco-fee of $1,000 per tonne on the burial of industrial toxic waste. This measure aims
to put an end to Blainville's status as a low-cost dump, discourage cross-border transfers and force
decontamination at source.

The discussions made it possible to re-examine the democratic injustice of the Quebec Loi 93 (Bill
93), imposed under parliamentary gag order to force municipal expropriation against the unanimous
verdict of report BAPE 371. The MP was also very critical of the federal government's posture at CCE
(Commission for Environmental Cooperation), which refuses to respond on cadmium (320x the standard)
to hide behind a procedural loophole.

The meeting resulted in specific operational steps:  parliamentary initiatives planned in December
2026 in synchronization with the international deadline of the CCE, the establishment of an Advisory
Council / permanent monitoring committee in January 2027, and the upcoming convening of an expanded
bilateral working session.

The MP praised the autonomy and rigor of the rising citizen generation and confirmed her wish to
involve youth in the future challenges of the constituency. The presence at the table of Édith Mora
Castelan, citizen expert and field watchdog in residual materials management, helped deepen
environmental compliance imperatives and rigorous protection for the peat bog basin.

### Meeting with Madeleine Chenette, federal deputy of Thérèse-De Blainville
Parliamentary follow-up meeting scheduled for November 2026 with the federal MP representing the
constituency in the House of Commons of Canada. The discussions will focus on the legal deadline of
November 10, 2026 (mandatory ministerial response to petition 546 from the OAG) and Canada's
cooperation under article 24.28 of the ACEUM (Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement) before the
Secretariat of the CCE.

## Multilateral Dialogue & International Representations

The citizen diplomacy exercised by William Guindon transcends local borders to address the
monitoring bodies of international environmental and human rights treaties:

#### Commission de coopération environnementale (CCE)
Submission of communication SEM-26-003, positive admissibility under art. 24.27(2)(3) and legal
response refuting Canada's inadmissibility.

#### United Nations (Special Rapporteur)
Urgent appeal and citizen brief transmitted to Dr. Marcos A. Orellana, UN Special Rapporteur on
toxic substances and human rights.

#### Environmental Commissioner (BVG)
Public Audit Petition No. 0546 filed with Jerry DeMarco under Section 22 of the Auditor General Act.

### Ethical principles of democratic dialogue
- Strict partisan independence: William Guindon does not campaign for any political party and finances
-   his work independently.
- Primacy of verifiable data: All exchanges are based on scientific data and the 222 supporting
-   documents placed in the public register.
- Full transparency: The commitments and directions decided during the work sessions are the subject
-   of verifiable public reports.

Title : Truth and Reconciliation · Grande Tourbière — William Guindon
==============================================================================

# William Guindon
Quebec environmental activist recognized for his commitment to the protection of ecosystems and the
rights of future generations. At the age of 15, he brought the Grande Tourbière de Blainville (Great
Blainville Peat Bog) file before the Commission de coopération environnementale (ACEUM) and the
United Nations.
> “It’s too late. The Blainville peat bog is already dead under your bulldozers. » — William Guindon,
> Le Devoir, April 8, 2025

### Recognition of unceded traditional territory
I recognize with deep respect that the Grande Tourbière de Blainville and the lands of the Lower
Laurentians on which my alert and research efforts are deployed are an integral part of the unceded
traditional territory of the Kanien'kehá:ka (Mohawk) nation, millennial guardian of the lands and
waters of Tiohtià:ke and the Mille-Îles River basin. This natural space also constitutes a historic
place of exchanges and shared passage with the Anishinaabeg nations and the entire Haudenosaunee
confederacy. I salute their continued stewardship of Mother Earth.
- Kanien'kehá:ka: “People of the Flint” (Mohawk Nation), guardians of the Eastern Gate of the
-   Haudenosaunee Confederacy.
- Tiohtià:ke: “Where the currents meet” (traditional Mohawk name for the archipelago and surrounding
-   river basins).
- Haudenosaunee: “The People of the Longhouse” (Six Nations Confederacy).

### The peat bog that remembers — Complete documentary file
Indigenous history (Kanien'kehá:ka, Anishinaabeg, Haudenosaunee), millennial ecology, water
territories of the Mille Îles River (Makigôtekw) and 6000 years of memory of the Grande Tourbière de
Blainville.

### Declaration of Intent & Civic Solidarity
“By bringing the safeguard of the Grande Tourbière de Blainville before the Commission de
coopération environnementale (file SEM-26-003) and by submitting before the UN Special Rapporteur,
my action is part of an inseparable solidarity with the defense of territories and respect for
ancestral rights. »

“Wetlands are not simple industrial lands to be sacrificed. Truth and reconciliation require
honoring memory, respecting treaties and rigorously protecting ecosystems for present and future
generations. »

#### Official Resources & Education

media.

Substances to denounce the expansion of Stablex in the Grande Tourbière. »

### “François Legault, you are destroying our future”
“Impacting open letter addressed to the Prime Minister of Quebec the day after the adoption under a
gag order of Projet de loi 93 (Bill 93) facilitating the expansion of the site. »

## Indigenous Nations Space

### Ancestral territory and environmental solidarity
The Grande Tourbière de Blainville and the St. Lawrence Lowlands are located on traditional unceded
territories of First Nations, including the Kanien'kehá:ka (Mohawk) and Anishinaabeg peoples.

#### Earth Care & Intergenerational Environmental Justice
“We do not inherit land from our parents, we borrow it from our children. » The SEM-26-003 file is
based on the shared responsibility to preserve rare wetlands against the burial of toxic and
industrial materials.

#### Youth and training
William Guindon grew up in the Laurentides region of Quebec. He completed his primary studies in
Blainville, where he distinguished himself in sixth grade by winning third place in the regional
final of the Expo-Sciences des Laurentides.

He continued his secondary studies in the Laurentians, where in 2023 he received the Méritas Prize
in the “ambassador — intellectual curiosity” category, then in 2024 the Méritas Prize “commitment
and humanism”. His academic results also earned him an exemption from the French and science final
exams. A member of the Royal Canadian Army Cadets, he reached the Silver Star level.

-   Imposition of Bill 93 in Quebec” ↗, on behalf of the Blainville community and as a representative
-   of future generations. The document argues that Projet de loi 93 violates Article 7 of the
-   Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms, Article 3 of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights
-   and UN General Assembly Resolution 76/300 recognizing the right to a healthy environment.

## The Stablex file

### A thousand-year-old ecosystem facing the landfill of hazardous waste
The Grande Tourbière de Blainville is a thousand-year-old ecosystem located in Blainville, included
by the Quebec Ministry of the Environment in the Atlas of Territories of Interest for Conservation
in the St. Lawrence Lowlands. The Stablex site, in operation since 1983, is the only center
authorized in Quebec for the final burial of materials resulting from the treatment by stabilization
and solidification of inorganic industrial residues.
- September 2023: The Bureau d'audiences publiques sur l'environnement (BAPE) concludes that the
-   expansion project (Cell 6) is “premature”.
- December 2023: Citizen analyzes reveal cadmium concentrations up to 320 times higher than
-   environmental standards in nearby surface waters.
- March 2025: The Quebec government adopts Projet de loi 93 under gag order, forcing the transfer of
-   land belonging to Ville de Blainville (City of Blainville), including 278,000 m² of wetlands, to
-   allow the expansion of the site.
- Blocked appeals: The law contains clauses excluding any legal appeal on the merits of the decision,
-   which the Centre québécois du droit de l'environnement (CQDE) describes as a serious violation of
-   the right to an effective remedy.
- Imports: Approximately 41% of the materials buried in Blainville come from outside Quebec, including
-   approximately 29% from the United States, which establishes the cross-border dimension of the
-   appeal filed with the CCE.

It is in this context that William Guindon chooses the path of law: rather than confrontation, he
mobilizes national and international mechanisms: BAPE, ACEUM, UN, to demand that Canada and Quebec
respect their own environmental obligations.

## Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)

### Understand everything about citizen initiatives and the SEM-26-003 file

### Who is William Guindon?
William Guindon (born in 2011 in Quebec) is a Quebec environmental activist and student in the
Laurentians. Author of the communication SEM-26-003 filed at the age of 14, he became at 15 the
first minor in the history of Environmental Submissions (SEM) to obtain a positive determination of
admissibility before the Commission de coopération environnementale (CCE / ACEUM). Consult his
detailed biography, the Stablex chronology or the press sheet.

### What is the SEM-26-003 file with CCE?
Communication SEM-26-003 (“Landfilling of hazardous materials in Blainville”) denounces the failure
to apply Canadian federal environmental laws (migratory birds, species at risk, cadmium discharge)
in the context of the expansion of the Stablex site into 278,000 m² of wetlands. Find the complete
chronology of the file.

### Why is Grande Tourbière de Blainville threatened?
The Grande Tourbière de Blainville is home to exceptional biodiversity and constitutes a major
carbon sink. The expansion project would allow millions of tons of toxic waste to be buried,
destroying 278,000 m² of rare ecosystems and contaminating waters with cadmium measured at more than
320 times the standards. View the perimeters on the interactive GIS map.

### What are the next steps after October 16, 2026?
On October 16, 2026, the Government of Canada is due to submit its official response. The CCE
Secretariat will then determine under Article 24.28 of ACEUM whether it recommends the development
of an independent Factual Record for vote by the Trinational Council of Ministers. Follow the
official count live.

encrypted channel below:

students or organizations. For any questions, suggestions or discussions relating to the Grande
Tourbière de Blainville and the SEM-26-003 file:

# Press Embargo & Media Relations
Fraternal practices, mutual respect of broadcast dates and open collaboration

## 1. Fraternal collaboration framework

This page presents the usual collaborative practices between William Guindon and news journalists.
It aims to promote the smooth transmission of documents of public interest, with mutual respect for
publication deadlines.

## 2. Simple embargo principles

- Definition: The embargo allows a press release or evidentiary document to be transmitted in advance
-   to allow journalists to study the file and prepare their coverage.
- Commitment of trust: When a deadline date and time are mentioned, the journalists undertake
-   fraternally not to publish before the agreed deadline.
- Clarity: Any transmission under embargo is expressly identified with its lifting time.

## 3. Interviews and direct collaboration

William directly receives all interview requests (virtual or face-to-face). In accordance with the
ethical rules applicable to minors, interviews are subject to customary parental consent.

To organize an interview or access media resources, go directly to the Official Press page ↗.

## 6. Selection of the press review and representativeness of the approaches

The press review presented on this site brings together a selection of reports, interviews and
surveys devoted to the Grande Tourbière de Blainville (Great Blainville Peat Bog) file and
SEM-26-003 communication.

Note of non-exhaustiveness: This selection is not exhaustive. The absence of an article, report or
media on this page in no way constitutes a judgment on its credibility, independence or journalistic
work. This site primarily highlights publications which allow the public and researchers to trace
the evolution of the scientific and legal data in the case.

If your report or publication is rigorous, respects the facts and ethics, but does not appear in the
press review, it is very possible that it has simply escaped my attention (I am not glued to the
continuous news!). Please feel free to forward the link to me directly at contact@williamguindon.me
so that it can be reviewed and included in the official registry.

This document is a standalone policy of the site. Each media outlet retains its editorial
independence. No signature is required for its publication on the site.

Title : Framework of Experts & Independence — William Guindon
